S1 / S2 Foundation 📈 Vector Calculus, Differential Equations and Transforms (MAT102)

3-Hour Exam Time Budget (20 Questions / 100 Marks Strategy)

Part A (10 Short Answers × 3 Marks = 30M): Allocate 5 mins per answer (50 mins total) — target ½ to 1 page highlighting key formula and core definition.
Part B (10 Essay Answers × 7 Marks = 70M): Allocate 12 mins per answer (120 mins total) — target 1.5 to 2 pages with schematic circuit/block diagrams and step-by-step derivations.
Strategy & Review Buffer (10 mins): 5 mins at start for question choice planning + 5 mins at end for question numbering and equation verification.

Course Code: MAT102 • 5 Modules • 26 Questions

Vector Calculus, Differential Equations and Transforms

Advanced engineering calculus and analytical transforms: First-order and second-order linear differential equations with constant/variable coefficients, method of variation of parameters, Cauchy-Euler equations, Laplace transforms & inverse transforms, convolution theorem, Fourier series expansions, and Fourier integral transforms.

Module 1

Ordinary Differential Equations, Bernoulli & Cauchy-Euler Equations

5 Elaborated University Questions & Answers

Question 1 3 Marks (Short Answer)
Short Answer ID: VCDET-Q1

Q1. Solve Bernoulli's differential equation dy/dx + y = y^2 (cos x - sin x). Q1. Solve Bernoulli's differential equation dy/dx + y = y^2 (cos x - sin x). Q1. Solve Bernoulli's differential equation dy/dx + y = y^2 (cos x - sin x).

Watch Video Solution
🏛️
KTU Exam Source: June 2023 University Exam (MAT102) • Original Paper Location: Part A • Question 1 (3 Marks)
👁️ View Question Paper
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$v = y^{-1} \implies \frac{dv}{dx} - v = \sin x - \cos x \implies \frac{1}{y} = C e^x - \sin x$$

Bernoulli Equation Transformation: Dividing by $y^n$ and substituting $v = y^{1-n}$ transforms the non-linear Bernoulli ODE into a standard first-order linear ODE solvable via integrating factor $e^{\int P dx}$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$v = y^{-1} \implies \frac{dv}{dx} - v = \sin x - \cos x \implies \frac{1}{y} = C e^x - \sin x$$

Bernoulli Equation Transformation: Dividing by $y^n$ and substituting $v = y^{1-n}$ transforms the non-linear Bernoulli ODE into a standard first-order linear ODE solvable via integrating factor $e^{\int P dx}$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$v = y^{-1} \implies \frac{dv}{dx} - v = \sin x - \cos x \implies \frac{1}{y} = C e^x - \sin x$$

Bernoulli Equation Transformation: Dividing by $y^n$ and substituting $v = y^{1-n}$ transforms the non-linear Bernoulli ODE into a standard first-order linear ODE solvable via integrating factor $e^{\int P dx}$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

A first-order non-linear differential equation of the form: $$\frac{dy}{dx} + P(x)y = Q(x)y^n$$ is known as **Bernoulli's equation**, where $P(x)$ and $Q(x)$ are continuous functions of $x$ (or constants) and $n \in \mathbb{R} \setminus \{0, 1\}$.

We are required to solve: $$\frac{dy}{dx} + y = y^2 (\cos x - \sin x)$$ Here, $P(x) = 1, Q(x) = \cos x - \sin x$, and the non-linear power is $n = 2$.

**Step 1: Divide by $y^2$ to Normalize the Non-Linear Term** Dividing the entire equation by $y^2$: $$y^{-2} \frac{dy}{dx} + y^{-1} = \cos x - \sin x \quad \text{--- (Equation 1)}$$

**Step 2: Linearizing Substitution** Let: $$v = y^{1 - n} = y^{1 - 2} = y^{-1} = \frac{1}{y}$$ Differentiate $v$ with respect to $x$ using the chain rule: $$\frac{dv}{dx} = -y^{-2} \frac{dy}{dx} \implies y^{-2} \frac{dy}{dx} = -\frac{dv}{dx}$$

**Step 3: Substitute into Equation (1)** $$-\frac{dv}{dx} + v = \cos x - \sin x$$ Multiply through by $-1$ to establish the standard linear form: $$\frac{dv}{dx} - v = \sin x - \cos x$$ This is a standard first-order linear ordinary differential equation in $v$ of the form: $$\frac{dv}{dx} + P_1(x)v = Q_1(x)$$ with $P_1(x) = -1$ and $Q_1(x) = \sin x - \cos x$.

**Step 4: Determine Integrating Factor (IF)** $$IF = e^{\int P_1(x) \, dx} = e^{\int -1 \, dx} = e^{-x}$$

**Step 5: Solve for $v(x)$** The general solution for the linear equation is: $$v \cdot IF = \int Q_1(x) \cdot IF \, dx + C$$ $$v e^{-x} = \int e^{-x} (\sin x - \cos x) \, dx + C$$ Notice that by the product rule of differentiation: $$\frac{d}{dx} \left[ e^{-x} \sin x \right] = -e^{-x} \sin x + e^{-x} \cos x = -e^{-x} (\sin x - \cos x)$$ Therefore: $$\int e^{-x} (\sin x - \cos x) \, dx = -e^{-x} \sin x$$ Substituting this back into the solution: $$v e^{-x} = -e^{-x} \sin x + C$$ Multiply through by $e^x$: $$v = -\sin x + C e^x = C e^x - \sin x$$

**Step 6: Resubstitute $v = 1/y$** $$\frac{1}{y} = C e^x - \sin x \implies y(x) = \frac{1}{C e^x - \sin x}$$ where $C$ is an arbitrary constant of integration.

A first-order non-linear differential equation of the form: $$\frac{dy}{dx} + P(x)y = Q(x)y^n$$ is known as **Bernoulli's equation**, where $P(x)$ and $Q(x)$ are continuous functions of $x$ (or constants) and $n \in \mathbb{R} \setminus \{0, 1\}$.

We are required to solve: $$\frac{dy}{dx} + y = y^2 (\cos x - \sin x)$$ Here, $P(x) = 1, Q(x) = \cos x - \sin x$, and the non-linear power is $n = 2$.

**Step 2: Linearizing Substitution** Let: $$v = y^{1 - n} = y^{1 - 2} = y^{-1} = \frac{1}{y}$$ Differentiate $v$ with respect to $x$ using the chain rule: $$\frac{dv}{dx} = -y^{-2} \frac{dy}{dx} \implies y^{-2} \frac{dy}{dx} = -\frac{dv}{dx}$$

**Step 3: Substitute into Equation (1)** $$-\frac{dv}{dx} + v = \cos x - \sin x$$ Multiply through by $-1$ to establish the standard linear form: $$\frac{dv}{dx} - v = \sin x - \cos x$$ This is a standard first-order linear ordinary differential equation in $v$ of the form: $$\frac{dv}{dx} + P_1(x)v = Q_1(x)$$ with $P_1(x) = -1$ and $Q_1(x) = \sin x - \cos x$.

**Step 4: Determine Integrating Factor (IF)** $$IF = e^{\int P_1(x) \, dx} = e^{\int -1 \, dx} = e^{-x}$$

**Step 5: Solve for $v(x)$** The general solution for the linear equation is: $$v \cdot IF = \int Q_1(x) \cdot IF \, dx + C$$ $$v e^{-x} = \int e^{-x} (\sin x - \cos x) \, dx + C$$ Notice that by the product rule of differentiation: $$\frac{d}{dx} \left[ e^{-x} \sin x \right] = -e^{-x} \sin x + e^{-x} \cos x = -e^{-x} (\sin x - \cos x)$$ Therefore: $$\int e^{-x} (\sin x - \cos x) \, dx = -e^{-x} \sin x$$ Substituting this back into the solution: $$v e^{-x} = -e^{-x} \sin x + C$$ Multiply through by $e^x$: $$v = -\sin x + C e^x = C e^x - \sin x$$

**Step 6: Resubstitute $v = 1/y$** $$\frac{1}{y} = C e^x - \sin x \implies y(x) = \frac{1}{C e^x - \sin x}$$ where $C$ is an arbitrary constant of integration.

A first-order non-linear differential equation of the form: $$\frac{dy}{dx} + P(x)y = Q(x)y^n$$ is known as **Bernoulli's equation**, where $P(x)$ and $Q(x)$ are continuous functions of $x$ (or constants) and $n \in \mathbb{R} \setminus \{0, 1\}$.

We are required to solve: $$\frac{dy}{dx} + y = y^2 (\cos x - \sin x)$$ Here, $P(x) = 1, Q(x) = \cos x - \sin x$, and the non-linear power is $n = 2$.

**Step 1: Divide by $y^2$ to Normalize the Non-Linear Term** Dividing the entire equation by $y^2$: $$y^{-2} \frac{dy}{dx} + y^{-1} = \cos x - \sin x \quad \text{--- (Equation 1)}$$

**Step 2: Linearizing Substitution** Let: $$v = y^{1 - n} = y^{1 - 2} = y^{-1} = \frac{1}{y}$$ Differentiate $v$ with respect to $x$ using the chain rule: $$\frac{dv}{dx} = -y^{-2} \frac{dy}{dx} \implies y^{-2} \frac{dy}{dx} = -\frac{dv}{dx}$$

**Step 3: Substitute into Equation (1)** $$-\frac{dv}{dx} + v = \cos x - \sin x$$ Multiply through by $-1$ to establish the standard linear form: $$\frac{dv}{dx} - v = \sin x - \cos x$$ This is a standard first-order linear ordinary differential equation in $v$ of the form: $$\frac{dv}{dx} + P_1(x)v = Q_1(x)$$ with $P_1(x) = -1$ and $Q_1(x) = \sin x - \cos x$.

**Step 4: Determine Integrating Factor (IF)** $$IF = e^{\int P_1(x) \, dx} = e^{\int -1 \, dx} = e^{-x}$$

**Step 5: Solve for $v(x)$** The general solution for the linear equation is: $$v \cdot IF = \int Q_1(x) \cdot IF \, dx + C$$ $$v e^{-x} = \int e^{-x} (\sin x - \cos x) \, dx + C$$ Notice that by the product rule of differentiation: $$\frac{d}{dx} \left[ e^{-x} \sin x \right] = -e^{-x} \sin x + e^{-x} \cos x = -e^{-x} (\sin x - \cos x)$$ Therefore: $$\int e^{-x} (\sin x - \cos x) \, dx = -e^{-x} \sin x$$ Substituting this back into the solution: $$v e^{-x} = -e^{-x} \sin x + C$$ Multiply through by $e^x$: $$v = -\sin x + C e^x = C e^x - \sin x$$

**Step 6: Resubstitute $v = 1/y$** $$\frac{1}{y} = C e^x - \sin x \implies y(x) = \frac{1}{C e^x - \sin x}$$ where $C$ is an arbitrary constant of integration.

Question 2 3 Marks (Short Answer)
Short Answer ID: VCDET-Q2

Q2. Solve the second order differential equation (D^2 - 4D + 4)y = 8e^(2x) + sin 2x. Q2. Solve the second order differential equation (D^2 - 4D + 4)y = 8e^(2x) + sin 2x. Q2. Solve the second order differential equation (D^2 - 4D + 4)y = 8e^(2x) + sin 2x.

Watch Video Solution
🏛️
KTU Exam Source: June 2023 University Exam (MAT102) • Original Paper Location: Part A • Question 2 (3 Marks)
👁️ View Question Paper
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$y = (C_1 + C_2 x) e^{2x} + 4x^2 e^{2x} + \frac{1}{8}\cos 2x$$

Repeated Roots & Resonant PI: When auxiliary root $m=a$ is repeated $k$ times, complementary function includes $(C_1 + C_2 x)e^{ax}$, and particular integral for $e^{ax}$ introduces resonant factor $\frac{x^k}{k!} e^{ax}$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$y = (C_1 + C_2 x) e^{2x} + 4x^2 e^{2x} + \frac{1}{8}\cos 2x$$

Repeated Roots & Resonant PI: When auxiliary root $m=a$ is repeated $k$ times, complementary function includes $(C_1 + C_2 x)e^{ax}$, and particular integral for $e^{ax}$ introduces resonant factor $\frac{x^k}{k!} e^{ax}$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$y = (C_1 + C_2 x) e^{2x} + 4x^2 e^{2x} + \frac{1}{8}\cos 2x$$

Repeated Roots & Resonant PI: When auxiliary root $m=a$ is repeated $k$ times, complementary function includes $(C_1 + C_2 x)e^{ax}$, and particular integral for $e^{ax}$ introduces resonant factor $\frac{x^k}{k!} e^{ax}$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

We are required to find the complete general solution of the second-order non-homogeneous ordinary differential equation with constant coefficients: $$(D^2 - 4D + 4)y = 8e^{2x} + \sin 2x$$ where $D = \frac{d}{dx}$ is the differential operator.

**Step 1: Determine the Complementary Function ($y_c$)** The corresponding homogeneous equation is $(D^2 - 4D + 4)y = 0$. The auxiliary equation is: $$m^2 - 4m + 4 = 0 \implies (m - 2)^2 = 0 \implies m = 2, \quad 2 \quad (\text{real repeated root})$$ For a repeated root $m = 2$ of multiplicity 2, the complementary function is: $$y_c = (C_1 + C_2 x) e^{2x}$$ where $C_1$ and $C_2$ are arbitrary constants.

**Step 2: Determine the Particular Integral ($y_p$)** By the superposition principle for linear systems, the particular integral is: $$y_p = \frac{1}{(D - 2)^2} [8e^{2x} + \sin 2x] = y_{p1} + y_{p2}$$

1. **Evaluation of $y_{p1} = \frac{1}{(D - 2)^2} [8e^{2x}]$:** Since $D = 2$ causes the denominator polynomial to vanish ($f(2) = 0$), we have a case of resonance. Because the root $m = 2$ has multiplicity $k = 2$: $$y_{p1} = 8 \cdot \frac{x^2}{2!} e^{2x} = 8 \cdot \frac{x^2}{2} e^{2x} = 4x^2 e^{2x}$$

2. **Evaluation of $y_{p2} = \frac{1}{D^2 - 4D + 4} [\sin 2x]$:** For a sinusoidal forcing function $\sin(ax)$ with $a = 2$, replace $D^2$ with $-a^2 = -(2^2) = -4$: $$y_{p2} = \frac{1}{-4 - 4D + 4} \sin 2x = \frac{1}{-4D} \sin 2x$$ Since $\frac{1}{D}$ denotes integration with respect to $x$: $$y_{p2} = -\frac{1}{4} \int \sin 2x \, dx = -\frac{1}{4} \left( -\frac{\cos 2x}{2} \right) = \frac{1}{8} \cos 2x$$

**Step 3: Form the Complete General Solution** $$y(x) = y_c + y_{p1} + y_{p2} = (C_1 + C_2 x) e^{2x} + 4x^2 e^{2x} + \frac{1}{8} \cos 2x$$

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

We are required to find the complete general solution of the second-order non-homogeneous ordinary differential equation with constant coefficients: $$(D^2 - 4D + 4)y = 8e^{2x} + \sin 2x$$ where $D = \frac{d}{dx}$ is the differential operator.

**Step 1: Determine the Complementary Function ($y_c$)** The corresponding homogeneous equation is $(D^2 - 4D + 4)y = 0$. The auxiliary equation is: $$m^2 - 4m + 4 = 0 \implies (m - 2)^2 = 0 \implies m = 2, \quad 2 \quad (\text{real repeated root})$$ For a repeated root $m = 2$ of multiplicity 2, the complementary function is: $$y_c = (C_1 + C_2 x) e^{2x}$$ where $C_1$ and $C_2$ are arbitrary constants.

**Step 2: Determine the Particular Integral ($y_p$)** By the superposition principle for linear systems, the particular integral is: $$y_p = \frac{1}{(D - 2)^2} [8e^{2x} + \sin 2x] = y_{p1} + y_{p2}$$

1. **Evaluation of $y_{p1} = \frac{1}{(D - 2)^2} [8e^{2x}]$:** Since $D = 2$ causes the denominator polynomial to vanish ($f(2) = 0$), we have a case of resonance. Because the root $m = 2$ has multiplicity $k = 2$: $$y_{p1} = 8 \cdot \frac{x^2}{2!} e^{2x} = 8 \cdot \frac{x^2}{2} e^{2x} = 4x^2 e^{2x}$$

2. **Evaluation of $y_{p2} = \frac{1}{D^2 - 4D + 4} [\sin 2x]$:** For a sinusoidal forcing function $\sin(ax)$ with $a = 2$, replace $D^2$ with $-a^2 = -(2^2) = -4$: $$y_{p2} = \frac{1}{-4 - 4D + 4} \sin 2x = \frac{1}{-4D} \sin 2x$$ Since $\frac{1}{D}$ denotes integration with respect to $x$: $$y_{p2} = -\frac{1}{4} \int \sin 2x \, dx = -\frac{1}{4} \left( -\frac{\cos 2x}{2} \right) = \frac{1}{8} \cos 2x$$

**Step 3: Form the Complete General Solution** $$y(x) = y_c + y_{p1} + y_{p2} = (C_1 + C_2 x) e^{2x} + 4x^2 e^{2x} + \frac{1}{8} \cos 2x$$

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

We are required to find the complete general solution of the second-order non-homogeneous ordinary differential equation with constant coefficients: $$(D^2 - 4D + 4)y = 8e^{2x} + \sin 2x$$ where $D = \frac{d}{dx}$ is the differential operator.

**Step 1: Determine the Complementary Function ($y_c$)** The corresponding homogeneous equation is $(D^2 - 4D + 4)y = 0$. The auxiliary equation is: $$m^2 - 4m + 4 = 0 \implies (m - 2)^2 = 0 \implies m = 2, \quad 2 \quad (\text{real repeated root})$$ For a repeated root $m = 2$ of multiplicity 2, the complementary function is: $$y_c = (C_1 + C_2 x) e^{2x}$$ where $C_1$ and $C_2$ are arbitrary constants.

**Step 2: Determine the Particular Integral ($y_p$)** By the superposition principle for linear systems, the particular integral is: $$y_p = \frac{1}{(D - 2)^2} [8e^{2x} + \sin 2x] = y_{p1} + y_{p2}$$

1. **Evaluation of $y_{p1} = \frac{1}{(D - 2)^2} [8e^{2x}]$:** Since $D = 2$ causes the denominator polynomial to vanish ($f(2) = 0$), we have a case of resonance. Because the root $m = 2$ has multiplicity $k = 2$: $$y_{p1} = 8 \cdot \frac{x^2}{2!} e^{2x} = 8 \cdot \frac{x^2}{2} e^{2x} = 4x^2 e^{2x}$$

2. **Evaluation of $y_{p2} = \frac{1}{D^2 - 4D + 4} [\sin 2x]$:** For a sinusoidal forcing function $\sin(ax)$ with $a = 2$, replace $D^2$ with $-a^2 = -(2^2) = -4$: $$y_{p2} = \frac{1}{-4 - 4D + 4} \sin 2x = \frac{1}{-4D} \sin 2x$$ Since $\frac{1}{D}$ denotes integration with respect to $x$: $$y_{p2} = -\frac{1}{4} \int \sin 2x \, dx = -\frac{1}{4} \left( -\frac{\cos 2x}{2} \right) = \frac{1}{8} \cos 2x$$

**Step 3: Form the Complete General Solution** $$y(x) = y_c + y_{p1} + y_{p2} = (C_1 + C_2 x) e^{2x} + 4x^2 e^{2x} + \frac{1}{8} \cos 2x$$

Question 3 7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Analytical Problem ID: VCDET-Q3

Q3. Solve the differential equation d^2y/dx^2 + y = sec x tan x using the method of variation of parameters. Q3. Solve the differential equation d^2y/dx^2 + y = sec x tan x using the method of variation of parameters. Q3. Solve the differential equation d^2y/dx^2 + y = sec x tan x using the method of variation of parameters.

Watch Video Solution
🏛️
KTU Exam Source: June 2023 University Exam (MAT102) • Original Paper Location: Part B • Question 12(a) (7 Marks)
👁️ View Question Paper
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$W = 1, \; u(x) = x - \tan x, \; v(x) = \ln|\sec x| \implies y = C_1 \cos x + C_2 \sin x + x \cos x + \sin x \ln|\sec x|$$

Variation of Parameters: For $y\'\' + y = R(x)$, basis solutions are $y_1 = \cos x, y_2 = \sin x$ with Wronskian $W = 1$. The formulas $u = -\int y_2 R / W \, dx$ and $v = \int y_1 R / W \, dx$ yield particular integrals for any non-elementary excitation.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$W = 1, \; u(x) = x - \tan x, \; v(x) = \ln|\sec x| \implies y = C_1 \cos x + C_2 \sin x + x \cos x + \sin x \ln|\sec x|$$

Variation of Parameters: For $y\'\' + y = R(x)$, basis solutions are $y_1 = \cos x, y_2 = \sin x$ with Wronskian $W = 1$. The formulas $u = -\int y_2 R / W \, dx$ and $v = \int y_1 R / W \, dx$ yield particular integrals for any non-elementary excitation.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$W = 1, \; u(x) = x - \tan x, \; v(x) = \ln|\sec x| \implies y = C_1 \cos x + C_2 \sin x + x \cos x + \sin x \ln|\sec x|$$

Variation of Parameters: For $y\'\' + y = R(x)$, basis solutions are $y_1 = \cos x, y_2 = \sin x$ with Wronskian $W = 1$. The formulas $u = -\int y_2 R / W \, dx$ and $v = \int y_1 R / W \, dx$ yield particular integrals for any non-elementary excitation.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

We are required to solve the second-order non-homogeneous linear differential equation: $$\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} + y = \sec x \tan x$$ using the **method of variation of parameters**. The standard undetermined coefficients method fails here because $\sec x \tan x$ possesses infinitely many linearly independent derivatives.

**Step 1: Find the Complementary Function ($y_c$)** The homogeneous equation is: $$\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} + y = 0$$ Auxiliary equation: $$m^2 + 1 = 0 \implies m = \pm i$$ The complementary function is: $$y_c = C_1 \cos x + C_2 \sin x$$ Identify the two linearly independent basis solutions: $$y_1(x) = \cos x, \quad y_2(x) = \sin x$$

**Step 2: Compute the Wronskian $W(y_1, y_2)$** The Wronskian determinant is: $$W(x) = \begin{vmatrix} y_1 & y_2 \\ y_1' & y_2' \end{vmatrix} = \begin{vmatrix} \cos x & \sin x \\ -\sin x & \cos x \end{vmatrix} = (\cos x)(\cos x) - (\sin x)(-\sin x) = \cos^2 x + \sin^2 x = 1$$ Since $W(x) = 1 \neq 0$, the solutions are linearly independent for all $x \in \mathbb{R}$.

**Step 3: Variation of Parameters Formula for Particular Integral** The particular integral is assumed to have the form: $$y_p(x) = u(x) y_1(x) + v(x) y_2(x)$$ where: $$u(x) = -\int \frac{y_2(x) R(x)}{W(x)} \, dx, \quad v(x) = \int \frac{y_1(x) R(x)}{W(x)} \, dx$$ with the forcing term $R(x) = \sec x \tan x$.

**Step 4: Compute $u(x)$** $$u(x) = -\int \frac{(\sin x)(\sec x \tan x)}{1} \, dx = -\int \sin x \cdot \frac{1}{\cos x} \cdot \frac{\sin x}{\cos x} \, dx = -\int \frac{\sin^2 x}{\cos^2 x} \, dx = -\int \tan^2 x \, dx$$ Using the trigonometric identity $\tan^2 x = \sec^2 x - 1$: $$u(x) = -\int (\sec^2 x - 1) \, dx = -(\tan x - x) = x - \tan x$$

**Step 5: Compute $v(x)$** $$v(x) = \int \frac{(\cos x)(\sec x \tan x)}{1} \, dx = \int \cos x \cdot \frac{1}{\cos x} \cdot \tan x \, dx = \int \tan x \, dx = \ln|\sec x|$$

**Step 6: Assemble the Particular Integral $y_p(x)$** $$y_p(x) = u(x) y_1(x) + v(x) y_2(x) = (x - \tan x)\cos x + (\ln|\sec x|)\sin x$$ $$y_p(x) = x \cos x - \tan x \cos x + \sin x \ln|\sec x| = x \cos x - \sin x + \sin x \ln|\sec x|$$ Notice that the term $-\sin x$ is a scalar multiple of the basis solution $y_2(x)$ and can be absorbed into the arbitrary constant $C_2$ in the complementary function.

**Step 7: Complete General Solution** $$y(x) = y_c + y_p = C_1 \cos x + C_2 \sin x + x \cos x + \sin x \ln|\sec x|$$ where $C_1$ and $C_2$ are arbitrary constants.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

We are required to solve the second-order non-homogeneous linear differential equation: $$\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} + y = \sec x \tan x$$ using the **method of variation of parameters**. The standard undetermined coefficients method fails here because $\sec x \tan x$ possesses infinitely many linearly independent derivatives.

**Step 1: Find the Complementary Function ($y_c$)** The homogeneous equation is: $$\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} + y = 0$$ Auxiliary equation: $$m^2 + 1 = 0 \implies m = \pm i$$ The complementary function is: $$y_c = C_1 \cos x + C_2 \sin x$$ Identify the two linearly independent basis solutions: $$y_1(x) = \cos x, \quad y_2(x) = \sin x$$

**Step 2: Compute the Wronskian $W(y_1, y_2)$** The Wronskian determinant is: $$W(x) = \begin{vmatrix} y_1 & y_2 \\ y_1' & y_2' \end{vmatrix} = \begin{vmatrix} \cos x & \sin x \\ -\sin x & \cos x \end{vmatrix} = (\cos x)(\cos x) - (\sin x)(-\sin x) = \cos^2 x + \sin^2 x = 1$$ Since $W(x) = 1 \neq 0$, the solutions are linearly independent for all $x \in \mathbb{R}$.

**Step 3: Variation of Parameters Formula for Particular Integral** The particular integral is assumed to have the form: $$y_p(x) = u(x) y_1(x) + v(x) y_2(x)$$ where: $$u(x) = -\int \frac{y_2(x) R(x)}{W(x)} \, dx, \quad v(x) = \int \frac{y_1(x) R(x)}{W(x)} \, dx$$ with the forcing term $R(x) = \sec x \tan x$.

**Step 4: Compute $u(x)$** $$u(x) = -\int \frac{(\sin x)(\sec x \tan x)}{1} \, dx = -\int \sin x \cdot \frac{1}{\cos x} \cdot \frac{\sin x}{\cos x} \, dx = -\int \frac{\sin^2 x}{\cos^2 x} \, dx = -\int \tan^2 x \, dx$$ Using the trigonometric identity $\tan^2 x = \sec^2 x - 1$: $$u(x) = -\int (\sec^2 x - 1) \, dx = -(\tan x - x) = x - \tan x$$

**Step 5: Compute $v(x)$** $$v(x) = \int \frac{(\cos x)(\sec x \tan x)}{1} \, dx = \int \cos x \cdot \frac{1}{\cos x} \cdot \tan x \, dx = \int \tan x \, dx = \ln|\sec x|$$

**Step 6: Assemble the Particular Integral $y_p(x)$** $$y_p(x) = u(x) y_1(x) + v(x) y_2(x) = (x - \tan x)\cos x + (\ln|\sec x|)\sin x$$ $$y_p(x) = x \cos x - \tan x \cos x + \sin x \ln|\sec x| = x \cos x - \sin x + \sin x \ln|\sec x|$$ Notice that the term $-\sin x$ is a scalar multiple of the basis solution $y_2(x)$ and can be absorbed into the arbitrary constant $C_2$ in the complementary function.

**Step 7: Complete General Solution** $$y(x) = y_c + y_p = C_1 \cos x + C_2 \sin x + x \cos x + \sin x \ln|\sec x|$$ where $C_1$ and $C_2$ are arbitrary constants.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

We are required to solve the second-order non-homogeneous linear differential equation: $$\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} + y = \sec x \tan x$$ using the **method of variation of parameters**. The standard undetermined coefficients method fails here because $\sec x \tan x$ possesses infinitely many linearly independent derivatives.

**Step 1: Find the Complementary Function ($y_c$)** The homogeneous equation is: $$\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} + y = 0$$ Auxiliary equation: $$m^2 + 1 = 0 \implies m = \pm i$$ The complementary function is: $$y_c = C_1 \cos x + C_2 \sin x$$ Identify the two linearly independent basis solutions: $$y_1(x) = \cos x, \quad y_2(x) = \sin x$$

**Step 2: Compute the Wronskian $W(y_1, y_2)$** The Wronskian determinant is: $$W(x) = \begin{vmatrix} y_1 & y_2 \\ y_1' & y_2' \end{vmatrix} = \begin{vmatrix} \cos x & \sin x \\ -\sin x & \cos x \end{vmatrix} = (\cos x)(\cos x) - (\sin x)(-\sin x) = \cos^2 x + \sin^2 x = 1$$ Since $W(x) = 1 \neq 0$, the solutions are linearly independent for all $x \in \mathbb{R}$.

**Step 3: Variation of Parameters Formula for Particular Integral** The particular integral is assumed to have the form: $$y_p(x) = u(x) y_1(x) + v(x) y_2(x)$$ where: $$u(x) = -\int \frac{y_2(x) R(x)}{W(x)} \, dx, \quad v(x) = \int \frac{y_1(x) R(x)}{W(x)} \, dx$$ with the forcing term $R(x) = \sec x \tan x$.

**Step 4: Compute $u(x)$** $$u(x) = -\int \frac{(\sin x)(\sec x \tan x)}{1} \, dx = -\int \sin x \cdot \frac{1}{\cos x} \cdot \frac{\sin x}{\cos x} \, dx = -\int \frac{\sin^2 x}{\cos^2 x} \, dx = -\int \tan^2 x \, dx$$ Using the trigonometric identity $\tan^2 x = \sec^2 x - 1$: $$u(x) = -\int (\sec^2 x - 1) \, dx = -(\tan x - x) = x - \tan x$$

**Step 5: Compute $v(x)$** $$v(x) = \int \frac{(\cos x)(\sec x \tan x)}{1} \, dx = \int \cos x \cdot \frac{1}{\cos x} \cdot \tan x \, dx = \int \tan x \, dx = \ln|\sec x|$$

**Step 6: Assemble the Particular Integral $y_p(x)$** $$y_p(x) = u(x) y_1(x) + v(x) y_2(x) = (x - \tan x)\cos x + (\ln|\sec x|)\sin x$$ $$y_p(x) = x \cos x - \tan x \cos x + \sin x \ln|\sec x| = x \cos x - \sin x + \sin x \ln|\sec x|$$ Notice that the term $-\sin x$ is a scalar multiple of the basis solution $y_2(x)$ and can be absorbed into the arbitrary constant $C_2$ in the complementary function.

**Step 7: Complete General Solution** $$y(x) = y_c + y_p = C_1 \cos x + C_2 \sin x + x \cos x + \sin x \ln|\sec x|$$ where $C_1$ and $C_2$ are arbitrary constants.

Question 4 7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Analytical Problem ID: VCDET-Q4

Q4. Solve the Cauchy-Euler differential equation: x^2 d^2y/dx^2 - 3x dy/dx + 4y = 2x^2 using the transformation x = e^z. Q4. Solve the Cauchy-Euler differential equation: x^2 d^2y/dx^2 - 3x dy/dx + 4y = 2x^2 using the transformation x = e^z. Q4. Solve the Cauchy-Euler differential equation: x^2 d^2y/dx^2 - 3x dy/dx + 4y = 2x^2 using the transformation x = e^z.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$[D_z(D_z-1) - 3D_z + 4]y = 2e^{2z} \implies y(x) = x^2 [C_1 + C_2 \ln x + (\ln x)^2]$$

Cauchy-Euler Reduction: Setting $x = e^z$ converts variable-coefficient Cauchy-Euler equations into constant-coefficient ODEs in $z$, where $x^k \frac{d^k y}{dx^k} = D_z(D_z - 1)\dots(D_z - k + 1)y$.

Cauchy-Euler Reduction: Setting $x = e^z$ converts variable-coefficient Cauchy-Euler equations into constant-coefficient ODEs in $z$, where $x^k \frac{d^k y}{dx^k} = D_z(D_z - 1)\dots(D_z - k + 1)y$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

We are required to solve the second-order Cauchy-Euler (equidimensional) differential equation: $$x^2 \frac{d^2y}{dx^2} - 3x \frac{dy}{dx} + 4y = 2x^2 \quad (x > 0)$$ using the logarithmic transformation $x = e^z$.

**Step 1: Coordinate Transformation to Constant Coefficients** Let: $$x = e^z \iff z = \ln x$$ Let $D_z = \frac{d}{dz}$ denote the derivative operator with respect to the new independent variable $z$. By the chain rule: $$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{dy}{dz} \frac{dz}{dx} = \frac{1}{x} \frac{dy}{dz} \implies x \frac{dy}{dx} = D_z y$$ For the second derivative: $$\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = \frac{d}{dx}\left( \frac{1}{x} \frac{dy}{dz} \right) = -\frac{1}{x^2} \frac{dy}{dz} + \frac{1}{x} \frac{d}{dz}\left(\frac{dy}{dz}\right)\frac{dz}{dx} = \frac{1}{x^2}\left( \frac{d^2y}{dz^2} - \frac{dy}{dz} \right)$$ $$x^2 \frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = D_z(D_z - 1)y$$

Also express the right-hand side excitation in terms of $z$: $$2x^2 = 2(e^z)^2 = 2e^{2z}$$

**Step 2: Formulate the Transformed ODE in $z$** Substitute these operator representations into the differential equation: $$[D_z(D_z - 1) - 3D_z + 4]y = 2e^{2z}$$ $$(D_z^2 - D_z - 3D_z + 4)y = 2e^{2z} \implies (D_z^2 - 4D_z + 4)y = 2e^{2z}$$ This is now a linear ODE with constant coefficients.

**Step 3: Complementary Function $y_c(z)$** Auxiliary equation: $$m^2 - 4m + 4 = 0 \implies (m - 2)^2 = 0 \implies m = 2, \quad 2$$ The complementary function is: $$y_c(z) = (C_1 + C_2 z) e^{2z}$$

**Step 4: Particular Integral $y_p(z)$** $$y_p(z) = \frac{1}{(D_z - 2)^2} [2e^{2z}]$$ Since $D_z = 2$ is a root of multiplicity $k = 2$ of the denominator operator: $$y_p(z) = 2 \cdot \frac{z^2}{2!} e^{2z} = 2 \cdot \frac{z^2}{2} e^{2z} = z^2 e^{2z}$$

**Step 5: General Solution in $z$** $$y(z) = y_c(z) + y_p(z) = (C_1 + C_2 z) e^{2z} + z^2 e^{2z} = (C_1 + C_2 z + z^2) e^{2z}$$

**Step 6: Transform back to the original variable $x$** Substitute $z = \ln x$ and $e^{2z} = (e^z)^2 = x^2$: $$y(x) = [C_1 + C_2 \ln x + (\ln x)^2] x^2$$ where $C_1$ and $C_2$ are arbitrary constants.

We are required to solve the second-order Cauchy-Euler (equidimensional) differential equation: $$x^2 \frac{d^2y}{dx^2} - 3x \frac{dy}{dx} + 4y = 2x^2 \quad (x > 0)$$ using the logarithmic transformation $x = e^z$.

**Step 1: Coordinate Transformation to Constant Coefficients** Let: $$x = e^z \iff z = \ln x$$ Let $D_z = \frac{d}{dz}$ denote the derivative operator with respect to the new independent variable $z$. By the chain rule: $$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{dy}{dz} \frac{dz}{dx} = \frac{1}{x} \frac{dy}{dz} \implies x \frac{dy}{dx} = D_z y$$ For the second derivative: $$\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = \frac{d}{dx}\left( \frac{1}{x} \frac{dy}{dz} \right) = -\frac{1}{x^2} \frac{dy}{dz} + \frac{1}{x} \frac{d}{dz}\left(\frac{dy}{dz}\right)\frac{dz}{dx} = \frac{1}{x^2}\left( \frac{d^2y}{dz^2} - \frac{dy}{dz} \right)$$ $$x^2 \frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = D_z(D_z - 1)y$$

Also express the right-hand side excitation in terms of $z$: $$2x^2 = 2(e^z)^2 = 2e^{2z}$$

**Step 2: Formulate the Transformed ODE in $z$** Substitute these operator representations into the differential equation: $$[D_z(D_z - 1) - 3D_z + 4]y = 2e^{2z}$$ $$(D_z^2 - D_z - 3D_z + 4)y = 2e^{2z} \implies (D_z^2 - 4D_z + 4)y = 2e^{2z}$$ This is now a linear ODE with constant coefficients.

**Step 3: Complementary Function $y_c(z)$** Auxiliary equation: $$m^2 - 4m + 4 = 0 \implies (m - 2)^2 = 0 \implies m = 2, \quad 2$$ The complementary function is: $$y_c(z) = (C_1 + C_2 z) e^{2z}$$

**Step 4: Particular Integral $y_p(z)$** $$y_p(z) = \frac{1}{(D_z - 2)^2} [2e^{2z}]$$ Since $D_z = 2$ is a root of multiplicity $k = 2$ of the denominator operator: $$y_p(z) = 2 \cdot \frac{z^2}{2!} e^{2z} = 2 \cdot \frac{z^2}{2} e^{2z} = z^2 e^{2z}$$

**Step 5: General Solution in $z$** $$y(z) = y_c(z) + y_p(z) = (C_1 + C_2 z) e^{2z} + z^2 e^{2z} = (C_1 + C_2 z + z^2) e^{2z}$$

**Step 6: Transform back to the original variable $x$** Substitute $z = \ln x$ and $e^{2z} = (e^z)^2 = x^2$: $$y(x) = [C_1 + C_2 \ln x + (\ln x)^2] x^2$$ where $C_1$ and $C_2$ are arbitrary constants.

**Step 5: General Solution in $z$** $$y(z) = y_c(z) + y_p(z) = (C_1 + C_2 z) e^{2z} + z^2 e^{2z} = (C_1 + C_2 z + z^2) e^{2z}$$

**Step 6: Transform back to the original variable $x$** Substitute $z = \ln x$ and $e^{2z} = (e^z)^2 = x^2$: $$y(x) = [C_1 + C_2 \ln x + (\ln x)^2] x^2$$ where $C_1$ and $C_2$ are arbitrary constants.

Question 5 7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Analytical Problem ID: VCDET-Q5

Q5. Solve the non-homogeneous higher order linear differential equation: (D^2 - 2D + 1)y = x e^x sin x using the exponential shift operator. Q5. Solve the non-homogeneous higher order linear differential equation: (D^2 - 2D + 1)y = x e^x sin x using the exponential shift operator. Q5. Solve the non-homogeneous higher order linear differential equation: (D^2 - 2D + 1)y = x e^x sin x using the exponential shift operator.

Watch Video Solution
🏛️
KTU Exam Source: June 2023 University Exam (MAT102) • Original Paper Location: Part B • Question 12(a) (7 Marks)
👁️ View Question Paper
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$y_p = e^x \frac{1}{D^2}[x \sin x] = -e^x(x\sin x + 2\cos x) \implies y = (C_1 + C_2 x)e^x - e^x(x\sin x + 2\cos x)$$

Exponential Shift Theorem: Shifting $e^{ax}$ outside the differential operator via $f(D)[e^{ax} V] = e^{ax} f(D+a)V$ eliminates the exponential factor and reduces operator power.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$y_p = e^x \frac{1}{D^2}[x \sin x] = -e^x(x\sin x + 2\cos x) \implies y = (C_1 + C_2 x)e^x - e^x(x\sin x + 2\cos x)$$

Exponential Shift Theorem: Shifting $e^{ax}$ outside the differential operator via $f(D)[e^{ax} V] = e^{ax} f(D+a)V$ eliminates the exponential factor and reduces operator power.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$y_p = e^x \frac{1}{D^2}[x \sin x] = -e^x(x\sin x + 2\cos x) \implies y = (C_1 + C_2 x)e^x - e^x(x\sin x + 2\cos x)$$

Exponential Shift Theorem: Shifting $e^{ax}$ outside the differential operator via $f(D)[e^{ax} V] = e^{ax} f(D+a)V$ eliminates the exponential factor and reduces operator power.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

We are required to solve the non-homogeneous higher order linear differential equation: $$(D^2 - 2D + 1)y = x e^x \sin x$$ where $D = \frac{d}{dx}$.

**Step 1: Determine the Complementary Function ($y_c$)** Auxiliary equation: $$m^2 - 2m + 1 = 0 \implies (m - 1)^2 = 0 \implies m = 1, \quad 1 \quad (\text{repeated root})$$ The complementary function is: $$y_c = (C_1 + C_2 x) e^x$$ where $C_1$ and $C_2$ are arbitrary constants.

**Step 2: Determine the Particular Integral ($y_p$)** $$y_p = \frac{1}{(D - 1)^2} [x e^x \sin x]$$

**Step 3: Apply the Exponential Shift Theorem** The fundamental operator shift identity states: $$\frac{1}{f(D)} [e^{ax} V(x)] = e^{ax} \frac{1}{f(D + a)} [V(x)]$$ Here $a = 1$ and $V(x) = x \sin x$. Replacing $D$ by $D + 1$: $$y_p = e^x \frac{1}{((D + 1) - 1)^2} [x \sin x] = e^x \frac{1}{D^2} [x \sin x]$$ The differential operator $\frac{1}{D^2}$ denotes two successive indefinite integrations with respect to $x$.

**Step 4: Perform Successive Integrations** - **First Integration ($\frac{1}{D}[x \sin x]$):** Integrate by parts: $\int u v' \, dx = u v - \int u' v \, dx$. Let $u = x \implies u' = 1$. Let $v' = \sin x \implies v = -\cos x$. $$\frac{1}{D}[x \sin x] = \int x \sin x \, dx = x(-\cos x) - \int 1 \cdot (-\cos x) \, dx = -x \cos x + \int \cos x \, dx$$ $$\frac{1}{D}[x \sin x] = -x \cos x + \sin x$$

- **Second Integration ($\frac{1}{D^2}[x \sin x] = \frac{1}{D}[-x \cos x + \sin x]$):** $$\frac{1}{D^2}[x \sin x] = \int (-x \cos x + \sin x) \, dx = -\int x \cos x \, dx + \int \sin x \, dx$$ Integrate $\int x \cos x \, dx$ by parts: $$\int x \cos x \, dx = x \sin x - \int 1 \cdot \sin x \, dx = x \sin x - (-\cos x) = x \sin x + \cos x$$ Therefore: $$\frac{1}{D^2}[x \sin x] = -(x \sin x + \cos x) + (-\cos x) = -x \sin x - \cos x - \cos x = -x \sin x - 2\cos x$$

**Step 5: Assemble the Particular Integral and Complete General Solution** $$y_p = e^x (-x \sin x - 2\cos x) = -e^x (x \sin x + 2\cos x)$$ Combining the complementary function and particular integral: $$y(x) = y_c + y_p = (C_1 + C_2 x)e^x - e^x (x \sin x + 2\cos x)$$

We are required to solve the non-homogeneous higher order linear differential equation: $$(D^2 - 2D + 1)y = x e^x \sin x$$ where $D = \frac{d}{dx}$.

**Step 1: Determine the Complementary Function ($y_c$)** Auxiliary equation: $$m^2 - 2m + 1 = 0 \implies (m - 1)^2 = 0 \implies m = 1, \quad 1 \quad (\text{repeated root})$$ The complementary function is: $$y_c = (C_1 + C_2 x) e^x$$ where $C_1$ and $C_2$ are arbitrary constants.

**Step 2: Determine the Particular Integral ($y_p$)** $$y_p = \frac{1}{(D - 1)^2} [x e^x \sin x]$$

**Step 3: Apply the Exponential Shift Theorem** The fundamental operator shift identity states: $$\frac{1}{f(D)} [e^{ax} V(x)] = e^{ax} \frac{1}{f(D + a)} [V(x)]$$ Here $a = 1$ and $V(x) = x \sin x$. Replacing $D$ by $D + 1$: $$y_p = e^x \frac{1}{((D + 1) - 1)^2} [x \sin x] = e^x \frac{1}{D^2} [x \sin x]$$ The differential operator $\frac{1}{D^2}$ denotes two successive indefinite integrations with respect to $x$.

**Step 4: Perform Successive Integrations** - **First Integration ($\frac{1}{D}[x \sin x]$):** Integrate by parts: $\int u v' \, dx = u v - \int u' v \, dx$. Let $u = x \implies u' = 1$. Let $v' = \sin x \implies v = -\cos x$. $$\frac{1}{D}[x \sin x] = \int x \sin x \, dx = x(-\cos x) - \int 1 \cdot (-\cos x) \, dx = -x \cos x + \int \cos x \, dx$$ $$\frac{1}{D}[x \sin x] = -x \cos x + \sin x$$

- **Second Integration ($\frac{1}{D^2}[x \sin x] = \frac{1}{D}[-x \cos x + \sin x]$):** $$\frac{1}{D^2}[x \sin x] = \int (-x \cos x + \sin x) \, dx = -\int x \cos x \, dx + \int \sin x \, dx$$ Integrate $\int x \cos x \, dx$ by parts: $$\int x \cos x \, dx = x \sin x - \int 1 \cdot \sin x \, dx = x \sin x - (-\cos x) = x \sin x + \cos x$$ Therefore: $$\frac{1}{D^2}[x \sin x] = -(x \sin x + \cos x) + (-\cos x) = -x \sin x - \cos x - \cos x = -x \sin x - 2\cos x$$

**Step 5: Assemble the Particular Integral and Complete General Solution** $$y_p = e^x (-x \sin x - 2\cos x) = -e^x (x \sin x + 2\cos x)$$ Combining the complementary function and particular integral: $$y(x) = y_c + y_p = (C_1 + C_2 x)e^x - e^x (x \sin x + 2\cos x)$$

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

We are required to solve the non-homogeneous higher order linear differential equation: $$(D^2 - 2D + 1)y = x e^x \sin x$$ where $D = \frac{d}{dx}$.

**Step 1: Determine the Complementary Function ($y_c$)** Auxiliary equation: $$m^2 - 2m + 1 = 0 \implies (m - 1)^2 = 0 \implies m = 1, \quad 1 \quad (\text{repeated root})$$ The complementary function is: $$y_c = (C_1 + C_2 x) e^x$$ where $C_1$ and $C_2$ are arbitrary constants.

**Step 2: Determine the Particular Integral ($y_p$)** $$y_p = \frac{1}{(D - 1)^2} [x e^x \sin x]$$

**Step 3: Apply the Exponential Shift Theorem** The fundamental operator shift identity states: $$\frac{1}{f(D)} [e^{ax} V(x)] = e^{ax} \frac{1}{f(D + a)} [V(x)]$$ Here $a = 1$ and $V(x) = x \sin x$. Replacing $D$ by $D + 1$: $$y_p = e^x \frac{1}{((D + 1) - 1)^2} [x \sin x] = e^x \frac{1}{D^2} [x \sin x]$$ The differential operator $\frac{1}{D^2}$ denotes two successive indefinite integrations with respect to $x$.

**Step 4: Perform Successive Integrations** - **First Integration ($\frac{1}{D}[x \sin x]$):** Integrate by parts: $\int u v' \, dx = u v - \int u' v \, dx$. Let $u = x \implies u' = 1$. Let $v' = \sin x \implies v = -\cos x$. $$\frac{1}{D}[x \sin x] = \int x \sin x \, dx = x(-\cos x) - \int 1 \cdot (-\cos x) \, dx = -x \cos x + \int \cos x \, dx$$ $$\frac{1}{D}[x \sin x] = -x \cos x + \sin x$$

- **Second Integration ($\frac{1}{D^2}[x \sin x] = \frac{1}{D}[-x \cos x + \sin x]$):** $$\frac{1}{D^2}[x \sin x] = \int (-x \cos x + \sin x) \, dx = -\int x \cos x \, dx + \int \sin x \, dx$$ Integrate $\int x \cos x \, dx$ by parts: $$\int x \cos x \, dx = x \sin x - \int 1 \cdot \sin x \, dx = x \sin x - (-\cos x) = x \sin x + \cos x$$ Therefore: $$\frac{1}{D^2}[x \sin x] = -(x \sin x + \cos x) + (-\cos x) = -x \sin x - \cos x - \cos x = -x \sin x - 2\cos x$$

**Step 5: Assemble the Particular Integral and Complete General Solution** $$y_p = e^x (-x \sin x - 2\cos x) = -e^x (x \sin x + 2\cos x)$$ Combining the complementary function and particular integral: $$y(x) = y_c + y_p = (C_1 + C_2 x)e^x - e^x (x \sin x + 2\cos x)$$

Vector Differentiation, Gradient, Divergence, Curl & Potentials

Question 6 3 Marks (Short Answer)
Short Answer ID: VCDET-Q6

Q6. Find the directional derivative of phi(x, y, z) = x^2 y z + 4 x z^2 at the point (1, -2, -1) in the direction of the vector a = 2i - j - 2k. Q6. Find the directional derivative of phi(x, y, z) = x^2 y z + 4 x z^2 at the point (1, -2, -1) in the direction of the vector a = 2i - j - 2k. Q6. Find the directional derivative of phi(x, y, z) = x^2 y z + 4 x z^2 at the point (1, -2, -1) in the direction of the vector a = 2i - j - 2k.

KTU Exam Source: June 2023 University Exam (MAT102) • Original Paper Location: Part A • Question 3 (3 Marks)

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\nabla \phi = 8\hat{i} - \hat{j} - 10\hat{k}, \quad \hat{u} = \frac{2\hat{i} - \hat{j} - 2\hat{k}}{3} \implies D_{\hat{u}}\phi = \nabla\phi \cdot \hat{u} = \frac{37}{3}$$

Directional Derivative: Represents the rate of change of a scalar field $\phi$ along a unit direction $\hat{u}$. Maximum rate of increase occurs along $\nabla\phi$ with magnitude $\|\nabla\phi\|$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\nabla \phi = 8\hat{i} - \hat{j} - 10\hat{k}, \quad \hat{u} = \frac{2\hat{i} - \hat{j} - 2\hat{k}}{3} \implies D_{\hat{u}}\phi = \nabla\phi \cdot \hat{u} = \frac{37}{3}$$

Directional Derivative: Represents the rate of change of a scalar field $\phi$ along a unit direction $\hat{u}$. Maximum rate of increase occurs along $\nabla\phi$ with magnitude $\|\nabla\phi\|$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The **directional derivative** of a differentiable scalar field $\phi(x, y, z)$ at a point $P$ in the direction of a non-zero vector $\vec{a}$ quantifies the instantaneous spatial rate of change of $\phi$ along that direction. It is formally calculated by taking the scalar dot product of the gradient vector $\nabla \phi$ evaluated at $P$ with the unit direction vector $\hat{u}$: $$D_{\hat{u}}\phi = \nabla \phi \cdot \hat{u} = \nabla \phi \cdot \frac{\vec{a}}{|\vec{a}|}$$

We are given the scalar field: $$\phi(x, y, z) = x^2 y z + 4 x z^2$$ at the point $P(1, -2, -1)$ in the direction of the vector $\vec{a} = 2\hat{i} - \hat{j} - 2\hat{k}$.

**Step 1: Compute the Gradient Vector $\nabla \phi$** $$\nabla \phi = \frac{\partial \phi}{\partial x}\hat{i} + \frac{\partial \phi}{\partial y}\hat{j} + \frac{\partial \phi}{\partial z}\hat{k}$$ Compute the first-order partial derivatives: $$\frac{\partial \phi}{\partial x} = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x^2 y z + 4 x z^2) = 2xyz + 4z^2$$ $$\frac{\partial \phi}{\partial y} = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(x^2 y z + 4 x z^2) = x^2 z$$ $$\frac{\partial \phi}{\partial z} = \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(x^2 y z + 4 x z^2) = x^2 y + 8xz$$

**Step 2: Evaluate $\nabla \phi$ at the Point $P(1, -2, -1)$** $$\left.\frac{\partial \phi}{\partial x}\right|_{(1, -2, -1)} = 2(1)(-2)(-1) + 4(-1)^2 = 4 + 4 = 8$$ $$\left.\frac{\partial \phi}{\partial y}\right|_{(1, -2, -1)} = (1)^2(-1) = -1$$ $$\left.\frac{\partial \phi}{\partial z}\right|_{(1, -2, -1)} = (1)^2(-2) + 8(1)(-1) = -2 - 8 = -10$$ Therefore: $$\nabla \phi_{(1, -2, -1)} = 8\hat{i} - \hat{j} - 10\hat{k}$$

**Step 3: Determine the Unit Direction Vector $\hat{u}$** The magnitude of $\vec{a} = 2\hat{i} - \hat{j} - 2\hat{k}$ is: $$|\vec{a}| = \sqrt{2^2 + (-1)^2 + (-2)^2} = \sqrt{4 + 1 + 4} = \sqrt{9} = 3$$ The unit vector is: $$\hat{u} = \frac{\vec{a}}{|\vec{a}|} = \frac{2\hat{i} - \hat{j} - 2\hat{k}}{3}$$

**Step 4: Compute the Directional Derivative** $$D_{\hat{u}}\phi = \nabla \phi \cdot \hat{u} = (8\hat{i} - \hat{j} - 10\hat{k}) \cdot \left( \frac{2\hat{i} - \hat{j} - 2\hat{k}}{3} \right)$$ $$= \frac{(8)(2) + (-1)(-1) + (-10)(-2)}{3} = \frac{16 + 1 + 20}{3} = \frac{37}{3}$$ The rate of increase of $\phi$ in the given direction is $\frac{37}{3}$ units per unit distance.

We are given the scalar field: $$\phi(x, y, z) = x^2 y z + 4 x z^2$$ at the point $P(1, -2, -1)$ in the direction of the vector $\vec{a} = 2\hat{i} - \hat{j} - 2\hat{k}$.

**Step 1: Compute the Gradient Vector $\nabla \phi$** $$\nabla \phi = \frac{\partial \phi}{\partial x}\hat{i} + \frac{\partial \phi}{\partial y}\hat{j} + \frac{\partial \phi}{\partial z}\hat{k}$$ Compute the first-order partial derivatives: $$\frac{\partial \phi}{\partial x} = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x^2 y z + 4 x z^2) = 2xyz + 4z^2$$ $$\frac{\partial \phi}{\partial y} = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(x^2 y z + 4 x z^2) = x^2 z$$ $$\frac{\partial \phi}{\partial z} = \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(x^2 y z + 4 x z^2) = x^2 y + 8xz$$

**Step 3: Determine the Unit Direction Vector $\hat{u}$** The magnitude of $\vec{a} = 2\hat{i} - \hat{j} - 2\hat{k}$ is: $$|\vec{a}| = \sqrt{2^2 + (-1)^2 + (-2)^2} = \sqrt{4 + 1 + 4} = \sqrt{9} = 3$$ The unit vector is: $$\hat{u} = \frac{\vec{a}}{|\vec{a}|} = \frac{2\hat{i} - \hat{j} - 2\hat{k}}{3}$$

**Step 4: Compute the Directional Derivative** $$D_{\hat{u}}\phi = \nabla \phi \cdot \hat{u} = (8\hat{i} - \hat{j} - 10\hat{k}) \cdot \left( \frac{2\hat{i} - \hat{j} - 2\hat{k}}{3} \right)$$ $$= \frac{(8)(2) + (-1)(-1) + (-10)(-2)}{3} = \frac{16 + 1 + 20}{3} = \frac{37}{3}$$ The rate of increase of $\phi$ in the given direction is $\frac{37}{3}$ units per unit distance.

Unit Normal & Tangent Plane: Gradient $\nabla f$ at any point on the level surface $f(x,y,z) = c$ defines the orthogonal surface normal. The tangent plane equation is $\nabla f \cdot (\vec{r} - \vec{r}_0) = 0$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\hat{n} = \frac{\nabla f}{|\nabla f|} = \frac{\hat{i} + \hat{j} + 2\hat{k}}{\sqrt{6}}, \quad \text{Tangent Plane: } x + y + 2z = 4$$

Unit Normal & Tangent Plane: Gradient $\nabla f$ at any point on the level surface $f(x,y,z) = c$ defines the orthogonal surface normal. The tangent plane equation is $\nabla f \cdot (\vec{r} - \vec{r}_0) = 0$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

A geometric surface defined implicitly by $f(x, y, z) = c$ represents a level surface of the scalar field $f$. The spatial gradient vector $\nabla f$ evaluated at any point $P(x_0, y_0, z_0)$ on the surface is strictly orthogonal (perpendicular) to the tangent plane of the surface at that point.

The **unit normal vector** to the surface at $P$ is defined by: $$\hat{n} = \frac{\nabla f}{|\nabla f|}$$ The corresponding equation of the **tangent plane** at $P(x_0, y_0, z_0)$ is: $$\nabla f \cdot [(x - x_0)\hat{i} + (y - y_0)\hat{j} + (z - z_0)\hat{k}] = 0$$

We are given the ellipsoidal surface: $$x^2 + y^2 + 2z^2 = 4$$ at the point $P(1, 1, 1)$.

**Step 1: Define the Scalar Level Function** $$f(x, y, z) = x^2 + y^2 + 2z^2 - 4 = 0$$

**Step 2: Compute the Gradient Vector $\nabla f$** $$\nabla f = \frac{\partial f}{\partial x}\hat{i} + \frac{\partial f}{\partial y}\hat{j} + \frac{\partial f}{\partial z}\hat{k} = 2x\hat{i} + 2y\hat{j} + 4z\hat{k}$$

**Step 3: Evaluate $\nabla f$ at Point $P(1, 1, 1)$** $$\vec{n} = \nabla f_{(1, 1, 1)} = 2(1)\hat{i} + 2(1)\hat{j} + 4(1)\hat{k} = 2\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + 4\hat{k}$$

**Step 4: Compute the Unit Normal Vector $\hat{n}$** Calculate the magnitude of $\vec{n}$: $$|\vec{n}| = \sqrt{2^2 + 2^2 + 4^2} = \sqrt{4 + 4 + 16} = \sqrt{24} = \sqrt{4 \times 6} = 2\sqrt{6}$$ The unit outward normal vector is: $$\hat{n} = \frac{2\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + 4\hat{k}}{2\sqrt{6}} = \frac{\hat{i} + \hat{j} + 2\hat{k}}{\sqrt{6}}$$

**Step 5: Determine the Tangent Plane Equation** The tangent plane equation at $(1, 1, 1)$ is: $$2(x - 1) + 2(y - 1) + 4(z - 1) = 0$$ $$2x - 2 + 2y - 2 + 4z - 4 = 0 \implies 2x + 2y + 4z = 8$$ Divide through by 2: $$x + y + 2z = 4$$

Question 8 7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)

Q8. Show that the vector field F = (2xy + z^3)i + x^2 j + 3xz^2 k is conservative and irrotational. Find its scalar potential phi(x, y, z) such that F = grad phi. Hence evaluate the work done in moving a particle from (1, -2, 1) to (3, 1, 4). Q8. Show that the vector field F = (2xy + z^3)i + x^2 j + 3xz^2 k is conservative and irrotational. Find its scalar potential phi(x, y, z) such that F = grad phi. Hence evaluate the work done in moving a particle from (1, -2, 1) to (3, 1, 4). Q8. Show that the vector field F = (2xy + z^3)i + x^2 j + 3xz^2 k is conservative and irrotational. Find its scalar potential phi(x, y, z) such that F = grad phi. Hence evaluate the work done in moving a particle from (1, -2, 1) to (3, 1, 4).

$$\nabla \times \vec{F} = \vec{0} \implies \phi = x^2 y + x z^3, \quad W = \phi(3, 1, 4) - \phi(1, -2, 1) = 201 - (-1) = 202$$

Conservative Field & Potential: An irrotational field ($\nabla \times \vec{F} = 0$) possesses a scalar potential $\vec{F} = \nabla\phi$. Line integrals are path-independent, reducing work done simply to $\phi(\text{end}) - \phi(\text{start})$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\nabla \times \vec{F} = \vec{0} \implies \phi = x^2 y + x z^3, \quad W = \phi(3, 1, 4) - \phi(1, -2, 1) = 201 - (-1) = 202$$

Conservative Field & Potential: An irrotational field ($\nabla \times \vec{F} = 0$) possesses a scalar potential $\vec{F} = \nabla\phi$. Line integrals are path-independent, reducing work done simply to $\phi(\text{end}) - \phi(\text{start})$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

We consider the three-dimensional vector field: $$\vec{F} = (2xy + z^3)\hat{i} + x^2\hat{j} + 3xz^2\hat{k}$$

We are required to: 1. Prove that $\vec{F}$ is conservative and irrotational. 2. Find the scalar potential function $\phi(x, y, z)$ such that $\vec{F} = \nabla \phi$. 3. Evaluate the work done in moving a particle under this force from point $A(1, -2, 1)$ to point $B(3, 1, 4)$.

--- ### Part 1: Prove that $\vec{F}$ is Irrotational and Conservative

A vector field is **irrotational** (and therefore conservative in simply connected domains) if its curl vanishes identically: $\nabla \times \vec{F} = \vec{0}$. Let $F_1 = 2xy + z^3$, $F_2 = x^2$, and $F_3 = 3xz^2$. $$\nabla \times \vec{F} = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ \frac{\partial}{\partial x} & \frac{\partial}{\partial y} & \frac{\partial}{\partial z} \\ 2xy + z^3 & x^2 & 3xz^2 \end{vmatrix}$$

Evaluate each component: - **$\hat{i}$-component:** $$\frac{\partial F_3}{\partial y} - \frac{\partial F_2}{\partial z} = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(3xz^2) - \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(x^2) = 0 - 0 = 0$$ - **$\hat{j}$-component:** $$-\left( \frac{\partial F_3}{\partial x} - \frac{\partial F_1}{\partial z} \right) = -\left( \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(3xz^2) - \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(2xy + z^3) \right) = -(3z^2 - 3z^2) = 0$$ - **$\hat{k}$-component:** $$\frac{\partial F_2}{\partial x} - \frac{\partial F_1}{\partial y} = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x^2) - \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(2xy + z^3) = 2x - 2x = 0$$

Thus: $$\nabla \times \vec{F} = 0\hat{i} + 0\hat{j} + 0\hat{k} = \vec{0}$$ Since $\nabla \times \vec{F} = \vec{0}$ everywhere in $\mathbb{R}^3$, $\vec{F}$ is strictly **irrotational** and **conservative**.

--- ### Part 2: Find the Scalar Potential $\phi(x, y, z)$

Since $\vec{F}$ is conservative, there exists a scalar potential $\phi$ such that: $$\nabla \phi = \vec{F} \implies \frac{\partial \phi}{\partial x}\hat{i} + \frac{\partial \phi}{\partial y}\hat{j} + \frac{\partial \phi}{\partial z}\hat{k} = (2xy + z^3)\hat{i} + x^2\hat{j} + 3xz^2\hat{k}$$

This yields three partial differential equations: 1. $\frac{\partial \phi}{\partial x} = 2xy + z^3 \quad \text{--- (1)}$ 2. $\frac{\partial \phi}{\partial y} = x^2 \quad \text{--- (2)}$ 3. $\frac{\partial \phi}{\partial z} = 3xz^2 \quad \text{--- (3)}$

Integrate Equation (1) with respect to $x$ (treating $y$ and $z$ as constants): $$\phi(x, y, z) = \int (2xy + z^3) \, dx = x^2 y + x z^3 + g(y, z) \quad \text{--- (4)}$$ where $g(y, z)$ is an arbitrary function of $y$ and $z$.

Differentiate Equation (4) with respect to $y$ and equate to Equation (2): $$\frac{\partial \phi}{\partial y} = x^2 + \frac{\partial g}{\partial y} = x^2 \implies \frac{\partial g}{\partial y} = 0$$ This implies $g(y, z)$ is independent of $y$, so $g(y, z) = h(z)$. Thus $\phi(x, y, z) = x^2 y + x z^3 + h(z)$.

Now differentiate with respect to $z$ and equate to Equation (3): $$\frac{\partial \phi}{\partial z} = 3xz^2 + h'(z) = 3xz^2 \implies h'(z) = 0 \implies h(z) = C \quad (\text{constant})$$

Taking $C = 0$ for the standard potential: $$\phi(x, y, z) = x^2 y + x z^3$$

--- ### Part 3: Evaluate Work Done

For any conservative force field, the work done along any curve $C$ connecting point $A$ to point $B$ is independent of the path and depends strictly on the potential difference at the boundaries: $$W = \int_A^B \vec{F} \cdot d\vec{r} = \int_A^B \nabla \phi \cdot d\vec{r} = \phi(B) - \phi(A)$$

Evaluate $\phi$ at the initial point $A(1, -2, 1)$: $$\phi(A) = \phi(1, -2, 1) = (1^2)(-2) + (1)(1^3) = -2 + 1 = -1$$

Evaluate $\phi$ at the final point $B(3, 1, 4)$: $$\phi(B) = \phi(3, 1, 4) = (3^2)(1) + (3)(4^3) = 9 + 3(64) = 9 + 192 = 201$$

Calculate the total work done: $$W = \phi(B) - \phi(A) = 201 - (-1) = 201 + 1 = 202 \text{ units}$$

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

We consider the three-dimensional vector field: $$\vec{F} = (2xy + z^3)\hat{i} + x^2\hat{j} + 3xz^2\hat{k}$$

We are required to: 1. Prove that $\vec{F}$ is conservative and irrotational. 2. Find the scalar potential function $\phi(x, y, z)$ such that $\vec{F} = \nabla \phi$. 3. Evaluate the work done in moving a particle under this force from point $A(1, -2, 1)$ to point $B(3, 1, 4)$.

--- ### Part 1: Prove that $\vec{F}$ is Irrotational and Conservative

A vector field is **irrotational** (and therefore conservative in simply connected domains) if its curl vanishes identically: $\nabla \times \vec{F} = \vec{0}$. Let $F_1 = 2xy + z^3$, $F_2 = x^2$, and $F_3 = 3xz^2$. $$\nabla \times \vec{F} = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ \frac{\partial}{\partial x} & \frac{\partial}{\partial y} & \frac{\partial}{\partial z} \\ 2xy + z^3 & x^2 & 3xz^2 \end{vmatrix}$$

Evaluate each component: - **$\hat{i}$-component:** $$\frac{\partial F_3}{\partial y} - \frac{\partial F_2}{\partial z} = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(3xz^2) - \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(x^2) = 0 - 0 = 0$$ - **$\hat{j}$-component:** $$-\left( \frac{\partial F_3}{\partial x} - \frac{\partial F_1}{\partial z} \right) = -\left( \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(3xz^2) - \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(2xy + z^3) \right) = -(3z^2 - 3z^2) = 0$$ - **$\hat{k}$-component:** $$\frac{\partial F_2}{\partial x} - \frac{\partial F_1}{\partial y} = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x^2) - \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(2xy + z^3) = 2x - 2x = 0$$

Thus: $$\nabla \times \vec{F} = 0\hat{i} + 0\hat{j} + 0\hat{k} = \vec{0}$$ Since $\nabla \times \vec{F} = \vec{0}$ everywhere in $\mathbb{R}^3$, $\vec{F}$ is strictly **irrotational** and **conservative**.

--- ### Part 2: Find the Scalar Potential $\phi(x, y, z)$

Since $\vec{F}$ is conservative, there exists a scalar potential $\phi$ such that: $$\nabla \phi = \vec{F} \implies \frac{\partial \phi}{\partial x}\hat{i} + \frac{\partial \phi}{\partial y}\hat{j} + \frac{\partial \phi}{\partial z}\hat{k} = (2xy + z^3)\hat{i} + x^2\hat{j} + 3xz^2\hat{k}$$

Integrate Equation (1) with respect to $x$ (treating $y$ and $z$ as constants): $$\phi(x, y, z) = \int (2xy + z^3) \, dx = x^2 y + x z^3 + g(y, z) \quad \text{--- (4)}$$ where $g(y, z)$ is an arbitrary function of $y$ and $z$.

Differentiate Equation (4) with respect to $y$ and equate to Equation (2): $$\frac{\partial \phi}{\partial y} = x^2 + \frac{\partial g}{\partial y} = x^2 \implies \frac{\partial g}{\partial y} = 0$$ This implies $g(y, z)$ is independent of $y$, so $g(y, z) = h(z)$. Thus $\phi(x, y, z) = x^2 y + x z^3 + h(z)$.

Now differentiate with respect to $z$ and equate to Equation (3): $$\frac{\partial \phi}{\partial z} = 3xz^2 + h'(z) = 3xz^2 \implies h'(z) = 0 \implies h(z) = C \quad (\text{constant})$$

Taking $C = 0$ for the standard potential: $$\phi(x, y, z) = x^2 y + x z^3$$

--- ### Part 3: Evaluate Work Done

For any conservative force field, the work done along any curve $C$ connecting point $A$ to point $B$ is independent of the path and depends strictly on the potential difference at the boundaries: $$W = \int_A^B \vec{F} \cdot d\vec{r} = \int_A^B \nabla \phi \cdot d\vec{r} = \phi(B) - \phi(A)$$

Evaluate $\phi$ at the initial point $A(1, -2, 1)$: $$\phi(A) = \phi(1, -2, 1) = (1^2)(-2) + (1)(1^3) = -2 + 1 = -1$$

Evaluate $\phi$ at the final point $B(3, 1, 4)$: $$\phi(B) = \phi(3, 1, 4) = (3^2)(1) + (3)(4^3) = 9 + 3(64) = 9 + 192 = 201$$

Calculate the total work done: $$W = \phi(B) - \phi(A) = 201 - (-1) = 201 + 1 = 202 \text{ units}$$

Analytical Problem ID: VCDET-Q9

Q9. Find constants a, b, c such that the vector field F = (x + 2y + az)i + (bx - 3y - z)j + (4x + cy + 2z)k is irrotational. Also show that F is solenoidal for all values of a, b, c. Q9. Find constants a, b, c such that the vector field F = (x + 2y + az)i + (bx - 3y - z)j + (4x + cy + 2z)k is irrotational. Also show that F is solenoidal for all values of a, b, c. Q9. Find constants a, b, c such that the vector field F = (x + 2y + az)i + (bx - 3y - z)j + (4x + cy + 2z)k is irrotational. Also show that F is solenoidal for all values of a, b, c.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\nabla \times \vec{F} = \vec{0} \implies a = 4, \; b = 2, \; c = -1; \quad \nabla \cdot \vec{F} = 1 - 3 + 2 = 0 \text{ (Solenoidal for all } a,b,c \text{)}$$

Irrotational and Solenoidal Fields: $\vec{F}$ is irrotational if $\nabla \times \vec{F} = \vec{0}$ (symmetric Jacobian). $\vec{F}$ is solenoidal if $\nabla \cdot \vec{F} = 0$ (divergence-free, zero net flux expansion).

$$\nabla \times \vec{F} = \vec{0} \implies a = 4, \; b = 2, \; c = -1; \quad \nabla \cdot \vec{F} = 1 - 3 + 2 = 0 \text{ (Solenoidal for all } a,b,c \text{)}$$

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\nabla \times \vec{F} = \vec{0} \implies a = 4, \; b = 2, \; c = -1; \quad \nabla \cdot \vec{F} = 1 - 3 + 2 = 0 \text{ (Solenoidal for all } a,b,c \text{)}$$

Irrotational and Solenoidal Fields: $\vec{F}$ is irrotational if $\nabla \times \vec{F} = \vec{0}$ (symmetric Jacobian). $\vec{F}$ is solenoidal if $\nabla \cdot \vec{F} = 0$ (divergence-free, zero net flux expansion).

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

We consider the vector field: $$\vec{F} = (x + 2y + az)\hat{i} + (bx - 3y - z)\hat{j} + (4x + cy + 2z)\hat{k}$$

We are required to: 1. Determine the scalar constants $a, b, c$ such that $\vec{F}$ is irrotational. 2. Prove that $\vec{F}$ is solenoidal for all real values of $a, b, c$.

--- ### Part 1: Determine Constants $a, b, c$ for Irrotationality

A vector field $\vec{F} = F_1\hat{i} + F_2\hat{j} + F_3\hat{k}$ is **irrotational** if and only if $\nabla \times \vec{F} = \vec{0}$: $$\nabla \times \vec{F} = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ \frac{\partial}{\partial x} & \frac{\partial}{\partial y} & \frac{\partial}{\partial z} \\ x + 2y + az & bx - 3y - z & 4x + cy + 2z \end{vmatrix} = \vec{0}$$

Expand the curl determinant: $$\hat{i} \left[ \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(4x + cy + 2z) - \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(bx - 3y - z) \right]$$ $$- \hat{j} \left[ \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(4x + cy + 2z) - \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(x + 2y + az) \right]$$ $$+ \hat{k} \left[ \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(bx - 3y - z) - \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(x + 2y + az) \right] = 0\hat{i} + 0\hat{j} + 0\hat{k}$$

Evaluate each bracketed partial derivative: 1. **$\hat{i}$-component:** $$\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(4x + cy + 2z) - \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(bx - 3y - z) = c - (-1) = c + 1$$ Setting this equal to zero: $$c + 1 = 0 \implies c = -1$$

2. **$\hat{j}$-component:** $$-\left[ \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(4x + cy + 2z) - \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(x + 2y + az) \right] = -(4 - a) = a - 4$$ Setting this equal to zero: $$a - 4 = 0 \implies a = 4$$

3. **$\hat{k}$-component:** $$\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(bx - 3y - z) - \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(x + 2y + az) = b - 2$$ Setting this equal to zero: $$b - 2 = 0 \implies b = 2$$

Thus, the vector field is irrotational when: $$a = 4, \quad b = 2, \quad c = -1$$

--- ### Part 2: Show that $\vec{F}$ is Solenoidal for all $a, b, c$

A vector field is **solenoidal** if its divergence is identically zero everywhere: $\nabla \cdot \vec{F} = 0$. Compute the divergence of $\vec{F}$: $$\nabla \cdot \vec{F} = \frac{\partial F_1}{\partial x} + \frac{\partial F_2}{\partial y} + \frac{\partial F_3}{\partial z}$$ $$\frac{\partial F_1}{\partial x} = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x + 2y + az) = 1$$ $$\frac{\partial F_2}{\partial y} = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(bx - 3y - z) = -3$$ $$\frac{\partial F_3}{\partial z} = \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(4x + cy + 2z) = 2$$

Summing these partial derivatives: $$\nabla \cdot \vec{F} = 1 + (-3) + 2 = 1 - 3 + 2 = 0$$

Notice that the divergence calculation is completely independent of the parameters $a, b$, and $c$. The algebraic sum of the direct coordinate partial derivatives evaluates identically to zero. Therefore, $\vec{F}$ is strictly **solenoidal** for all arbitrary real values of $a, b$, and $c$.

We consider the vector field: $$\vec{F} = (x + 2y + az)\hat{i} + (bx - 3y - z)\hat{j} + (4x + cy + 2z)\hat{k}$$

We are required to: 1. Determine the scalar constants $a, b, c$ such that $\vec{F}$ is irrotational. 2. Prove that $\vec{F}$ is solenoidal for all real values of $a, b, c$.

--- ### Part 1: Determine Constants $a, b, c$ for Irrotationality

A vector field $\vec{F} = F_1\hat{i} + F_2\hat{j} + F_3\hat{k}$ is **irrotational** if and only if $\nabla \times \vec{F} = \vec{0}$: $$\nabla \times \vec{F} = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ \frac{\partial}{\partial x} & \frac{\partial}{\partial y} & \frac{\partial}{\partial z} \\ x + 2y + az & bx - 3y - z & 4x + cy + 2z \end{vmatrix} = \vec{0}$$

Expand the curl determinant: $$\hat{i} \left[ \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(4x + cy + 2z) - \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(bx - 3y - z) \right]$$ $$- \hat{j} \left[ \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(4x + cy + 2z) - \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(x + 2y + az) \right]$$ $$+ \hat{k} \left[ \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(bx - 3y - z) - \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(x + 2y + az) \right] = 0\hat{i} + 0\hat{j} + 0\hat{k}$$

Evaluate each bracketed partial derivative: 1. **$\hat{i}$-component:** $$\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(4x + cy + 2z) - \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(bx - 3y - z) = c - (-1) = c + 1$$ Setting this equal to zero: $$c + 1 = 0 \implies c = -1$$

2. **$\hat{j}$-component:** $$-\left[ \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(4x + cy + 2z) - \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(x + 2y + az) \right] = -(4 - a) = a - 4$$ Setting this equal to zero: $$a - 4 = 0 \implies a = 4$$

3. **$\hat{k}$-component:** $$\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(bx - 3y - z) - \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(x + 2y + az) = b - 2$$ Setting this equal to zero: $$b - 2 = 0 \implies b = 2$$

Thus, the vector field is irrotational when: $$a = 4, \quad b = 2, \quad c = -1$$

--- ### Part 2: Show that $\vec{F}$ is Solenoidal for all $a, b, c$

A vector field is **solenoidal** if its divergence is identically zero everywhere: $\nabla \cdot \vec{F} = 0$. Compute the divergence of $\vec{F}$: $$\nabla \cdot \vec{F} = \frac{\partial F_1}{\partial x} + \frac{\partial F_2}{\partial y} + \frac{\partial F_3}{\partial z}$$ $$\frac{\partial F_1}{\partial x} = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x + 2y + az) = 1$$ $$\frac{\partial F_2}{\partial y} = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(bx - 3y - z) = -3$$ $$\frac{\partial F_3}{\partial z} = \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(4x + cy + 2z) = 2$$

Summing these partial derivatives: $$\nabla \cdot \vec{F} = 1 + (-3) + 2 = 1 - 3 + 2 = 0$$

Notice that the divergence calculation is completely independent of the parameters $a, b$, and $c$. The algebraic sum of the direct coordinate partial derivatives evaluates identically to zero. Therefore, $\vec{F}$ is strictly **solenoidal** for all arbitrary real values of $a, b$, and $c$.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

We consider the vector field: $$\vec{F} = (x + 2y + az)\hat{i} + (bx - 3y - z)\hat{j} + (4x + cy + 2z)\hat{k}$$

We are required to: 1. Determine the scalar constants $a, b, c$ such that $\vec{F}$ is irrotational. 2. Prove that $\vec{F}$ is solenoidal for all real values of $a, b, c$.

A vector field $\vec{F} = F_1\hat{i} + F_2\hat{j} + F_3\hat{k}$ is **irrotational** if and only if $\nabla \times \vec{F} = \vec{0}$: $$\nabla \times \vec{F} = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ \frac{\partial}{\partial x} & \frac{\partial}{\partial y} & \frac{\partial}{\partial z} \\ x + 2y + az & bx - 3y - z & 4x + cy + 2z \end{vmatrix} = \vec{0}$$

Expand the curl determinant: $$\hat{i} \left[ \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(4x + cy + 2z) - \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(bx - 3y - z) \right]$$ $$- \hat{j} \left[ \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(4x + cy + 2z) - \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(x + 2y + az) \right]$$ $$+ \hat{k} \left[ \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(bx - 3y - z) - \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(x + 2y + az) \right] = 0\hat{i} + 0\hat{j} + 0\hat{k}$$

Evaluate each bracketed partial derivative: 1. **$\hat{i}$-component:** $$\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(4x + cy + 2z) - \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(bx - 3y - z) = c - (-1) = c + 1$$ Setting this equal to zero: $$c + 1 = 0 \implies c = -1$$

2. **$\hat{j}$-component:** $$-\left[ \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(4x + cy + 2z) - \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(x + 2y + az) \right] = -(4 - a) = a - 4$$ Setting this equal to zero: $$a - 4 = 0 \implies a = 4$$

3. **$\hat{k}$-component:** $$\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(bx - 3y - z) - \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(x + 2y + az) = b - 2$$ Setting this equal to zero: $$b - 2 = 0 \implies b = 2$$

Thus, the vector field is irrotational when: $$a = 4, \quad b = 2, \quad c = -1$$

--- ### Part 2: Show that $\vec{F}$ is Solenoidal for all $a, b, c$

A vector field is **solenoidal** if its divergence is identically zero everywhere: $\nabla \cdot \vec{F} = 0$. Compute the divergence of $\vec{F}$: $$\nabla \cdot \vec{F} = \frac{\partial F_1}{\partial x} + \frac{\partial F_2}{\partial y} + \frac{\partial F_3}{\partial z}$$ $$\frac{\partial F_1}{\partial x} = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x + 2y + az) = 1$$ $$\frac{\partial F_2}{\partial y} = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(bx - 3y - z) = -3$$ $$\frac{\partial F_3}{\partial z} = \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(4x + cy + 2z) = 2$$

Summing these partial derivatives: $$\nabla \cdot \vec{F} = 1 + (-3) + 2 = 1 - 3 + 2 = 0$$

Notice that the divergence calculation is completely independent of the parameters $a, b$, and $c$. The algebraic sum of the direct coordinate partial derivatives evaluates identically to zero. Therefore, $\vec{F}$ is strictly **solenoidal** for all arbitrary real values of $a, b$, and $c$.

Question 10 7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Analytical Problem ID: VCDET-Q10

Q10. If r = x i + y j + z k and r = |r|, prove that: (i) grad(r^n) = n r^(n-2) r, (ii) div(r^n r) = (n+3)r^n, and (iii) Laplacian(r^n) = n(n+1)r^(n-2). Hence show that 1/r is harmonic for r != 0. Q10. If r = x i + y j + z k and r = |r|, prove that: (i) grad(r^n) = n r^(n-2) r, (ii) div(r^n r) = (n+3)r^n, and (iii) Laplacian(r^n) = n(n+1)r^(n-2). Hence show that 1/r is harmonic for r != 0.

$$\nabla(r^n) = n r^{n-2}\vec{r}, \quad \nabla \cdot (r^n \vec{r}) = (n+3)r^n, \quad \nabla^2(r^n) = n(n+1)r^{n-2} \implies \nabla^2(r^{-1}) = 0$$

Radial Field Vector Identities: Central fields have radial gradients. The Laplacian $\nabla^2(r^n) = n(n+1)r^{n-2}$ vanishes identically for $n = -1$, proving that the Coulomb/Newtonian potential $1/r$ satisfies Laplace\'s equation.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Let $\vec{r} = x\hat{i} + y\hat{j} + z\hat{k}$ be the position vector in three-dimensional Euclidean space $\mathbb{R}^3$, and let $r = |\vec{r}| = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2 + z^2}$ be its Euclidean magnitude (distance from the origin).

**Preliminary Partial Derivatives of $r$:** Since $r^2 = x^2 + y^2 + z^2$, differentiating implicitly with respect to $x$: $$2r \frac{\partial r}{\partial x} = 2x \implies \frac{\partial r}{\partial x} = \frac{x}{r}$$ By symmetry: $$\frac{\partial r}{\partial y} = \frac{y}{r}, \quad \frac{\partial r}{\partial z} = \frac{z}{r}$$ The gradient of the scalar distance $r$ is: $$\nabla r = \frac{\partial r}{\partial x}\hat{i} + \frac{\partial r}{\partial y}\hat{j} + \frac{\partial r}{\partial z}\hat{k} = \frac{x}{r}\hat{i} + \frac{y}{r}\hat{j} + \frac{z}{r}\hat{k} = \frac{\vec{r}}{r}$$

--- ### Part (i): Prove that $\nabla(r^n) = n r^{n-2} \vec{r}$

By the chain rule of multivariable calculus: $$\nabla(r^n) = \frac{d(r^n)}{dr} \nabla r$$ Since $\frac{d(r^n)}{dr} = n r^{n-1}$ and $\nabla r = \frac{\vec{r}}{r}$: $$\nabla(r^n) = (n r^{n-1}) \left( \frac{\vec{r}}{r} \right) = n r^{n-2} \vec{r}$$ Hence proved.

--- ### Part (ii): Prove that $\nabla \cdot (r^n \vec{r}) = (n + 3)r^n$

Using the standard vector identity for the divergence of a product of a scalar field $\phi$ and a vector field $\vec{A}$: $$\nabla \cdot (\phi \vec{A}) = \phi (\nabla \cdot \vec{A}) + (\nabla \phi) \cdot \vec{A}$$ Let $\phi = r^n$ and $\vec{A} = \vec{r}$: $$\nabla \cdot (r^n \vec{r}) = r^n (\nabla \cdot \vec{r}) + \nabla(r^n) \cdot \vec{r}$$

Now evaluate $\nabla \cdot \vec{r}$: $$\nabla \cdot \vec{r} = \frac{\partial x}{\partial x} + \frac{\partial y}{\partial y} + \frac{\partial z}{\partial z} = 1 + 1 + 1 = 3$$ From Part (i), substitute $\nabla(r^n) = n r^{n-2}\vec{r}$: $$\nabla(r^n) \cdot \vec{r} = (n r^{n-2} \vec{r}) \cdot \vec{r} = n r^{n-2} (\vec{r} \cdot \vec{r})$$ Since $\vec{r} \cdot \vec{r} = |\vec{r}|^2 = r^2$: $$\nabla(r^n) \cdot \vec{r} = n r^{n-2} (r^2) = n r^n$$ Substitute both terms: $$\nabla \cdot (r^n \vec{r}) = r^n (3) + n r^n = (n + 3)r^n$$ Hence proved.

--- ### Part (iii): Prove that $\nabla^2(r^n) = n(n + 1)r^{n-2}$ and Harmonicity of $1/r$

The Laplacian operator $\nabla^2$ is defined as the divergence of the gradient: $$\nabla^2(r^n) = \nabla \cdot [\nabla(r^n)]$$ From Part (i), $\nabla(r^n) = n r^{n-2} \vec{r}$. Therefore: $$\nabla^2(r^n) = \nabla \cdot (n r^{n-2} \vec{r}) = n [\nabla \cdot (r^{n-2} \vec{r})]$$ Apply the result of Part (ii) with the replacement of exponent $n$ by $m = n - 2$: $$\nabla \cdot (r^{n-2} \vec{r}) = ((n - 2) + 3) r^{n-2} = (n + 1)r^{n-2}$$ Multiply by the constant pre-factor $n$: $$\nabla^2(r^n) = n(n + 1)r^{n-2}$$ Hence proved.

**Deduction: Proof that $1/r$ is Harmonic ($r \neq 0$):** A scalar function $\psi$ is defined to be **harmonic** in a spatial region if it satisfies Laplace's equation: $$\nabla^2 \psi = 0$$ Let $\psi = \frac{1}{r} = r^{-1}$. Here $n = -1$. Substitute $n = -1$ into the general Laplacian formula: $$\nabla^2(r^{-1}) = (-1)(-1 + 1)r^{-1 - 2} = (-1)(0)r^{-3} = 0 \quad (\text{for } r \neq 0)$$ Since $\nabla^2(1/r) = 0$, the inverse distance field $1/r$ is strictly harmonic everywhere in $\mathbb{R}^3 \setminus \{(0,0,0)\}$. This confirms why the electrostatic potential of a point charge and the gravitational potential of a point mass satisfy Laplace's equation in source-free space.

Vector Integration, Green's, Gauss Divergence & Stokes' Theorems

Question 11 3 Marks (Short Answer)
Short Answer ID: VCDET-Q11

Q11. Using Green's theorem, evaluate the line integral oint_C [(x - y) dx + (x + y) dy] where C is the closed curve bounded by y = x^2 and y^2 = x traversed counter-clockwise. Q11. Using Green's theorem, evaluate the line integral oint_C [(x - y) dx + (x + y) dy] where C is the closed curve bounded by y = x^2 and y^2 = x traversed counter-clockwise. Q11. Using Green's theorem, evaluate the line integral oint_C [(x - y) dx + (x + y) dy] where C is the closed curve bounded by y = x^2 and y^2 = x traversed counter-clockwise.

Watch Video Solution
🏛️
KTU Exam Source: June 2023 University Exam (MAT102) • Original Paper Location: Part A • Question 5 (3 Marks)
👁️ View Question Paper
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\oint_C [(x-y)dx + (x+y)dy] = \iint_R (1 - (-1)) dx\,dy = 2 \int_0^1 (\sqrt{x} - x^2)dx = 2\left(\frac{1}{3}\right) = \frac{2}{3}$$

Green\'s Theorem in a Plane: Converts a closed line integral $\oint (Mdx + Ndy)$ into a double integral of $(\partial N/\partial x - \partial M/\partial y)$. When the integrand is constant 2, the integral simply equals $2 \times \text{enclosed area}$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\oint_C [(x-y)dx + (x+y)dy] = \iint_R (1 - (-1)) dx\,dy = 2 \int_0^1 (\sqrt{x} - x^2)dx = 2\left(\frac{1}{3}\right) = \frac{2}{3}$$

Green\'s Theorem in a Plane: Converts a closed line integral $\oint (Mdx + Ndy)$ into a double integral of $(\partial N/\partial x - \partial M/\partial y)$. When the integrand is constant 2, the integral simply equals $2 \times \text{enclosed area}$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\oint_C [(x-y)dx + (x+y)dy] = \iint_R (1 - (-1)) dx\,dy = 2 \int_0^1 (\sqrt{x} - x^2)dx = 2\left(\frac{1}{3}\right) = \frac{2}{3}$$

Green\'s Theorem in a Plane: Converts a closed line integral $\oint (Mdx + Ndy)$ into a double integral of $(\partial N/\partial x - \partial M/\partial y)$. When the integrand is constant 2, the integral simply equals $2 \times \text{enclosed area}$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

**Statement of Green's Theorem in a Plane:** Let $C$ be a positively oriented (counter-clockwise), piecewise smooth, simple closed curve in the $xy$-plane bounding a simply connected region $R$. If $M(x, y)$ and $N(x, y)$ are continuous functions having continuous first-order partial derivatives throughout $R$, then: $$\oint_C (M \, dx + N \, dy) = \iint_R \left( \frac{\partial N}{\partial x} - \frac{\partial M}{\partial y} \right) dx \, dy$$

We are required to evaluate: $$I = \oint_C [(x - y)\, dx + (x + y)\, dy]$$ around the closed boundary $C$ of the region bounded by $y = x^2$ and $y^2 = x$.

**Step 1: Identify Functions $M$ and $N$ and Their Partial Derivatives** $$M(x, y) = x - y \implies \frac{\partial M}{\partial y} = -1$$ $$N(x, y) = x + y \implies \frac{\partial N}{\partial x} = 1$$ Compute the integrand of the double integral: $$\frac{\partial N}{\partial x} - \frac{\partial M}{\partial y} = 1 - (-1) = 1 + 1 = 2$$

**Step 2: Determine the Region of Integration $R$** The boundary curves are: - Lower boundary: $y = x^2$ - Upper boundary: $y = \sqrt{x}$ (from $y^2 = x$) Intersection points: $$x^2 = \sqrt{x} \implies x^4 = x \implies x(x^3 - 1) = 0 \implies x = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad x = 1$$ The curves intersect at $(0, 0)$ and $(1, 1)$.

**Step 3: Evaluate the Double Integral** Applying Green's theorem: $$I = \iint_R 2 \, dx \, dy = 2 \iint_R dx \, dy = 2 \times (\text{Area of region } R)$$ Set up the iterated integral: $$\text{Area}(R) = \int_{x=0}^1 \left[ \int_{y=x^2}^{\sqrt{x}} dy \right] dx = \int_0^1 (\sqrt{x} - x^2) \, dx$$ Evaluate the single integral: $$\int_0^1 (x^{1/2} - x^2) \, dx = \left[ \frac{x^{3/2}}{3/2} - \frac{x^3}{3} \right]_0^1 = \left[ \frac{2}{3}x^{3/2} - \frac{1}{3}x^3 \right]_0^1 = \frac{2}{3} - \frac{1}{3} = \frac{1}{3}$$ Multiplying by the integrand value 2: $$I = 2 \left( \frac{1}{3} \right) = \frac{2}{3}$$

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

**Statement of Green's Theorem in a Plane:** Let $C$ be a positively oriented (counter-clockwise), piecewise smooth, simple closed curve in the $xy$-plane bounding a simply connected region $R$. If $M(x, y)$ and $N(x, y)$ are continuous functions having continuous first-order partial derivatives throughout $R$, then: $$\oint_C (M \, dx + N \, dy) = \iint_R \left( \frac{\partial N}{\partial x} - \frac{\partial M}{\partial y} \right) dx \, dy$$

We are required to evaluate: $$I = \oint_C [(x - y)\, dx + (x + y)\, dy]$$ around the closed boundary $C$ of the region bounded by $y = x^2$ and $y^2 = x$.

**Step 1: Identify Functions $M$ and $N$ and Their Partial Derivatives** $$M(x, y) = x - y \implies \frac{\partial M}{\partial y} = -1$$ $$N(x, y) = x + y \implies \frac{\partial N}{\partial x} = 1$$ Compute the integrand of the double integral: $$\frac{\partial N}{\partial x} - \frac{\partial M}{\partial y} = 1 - (-1) = 1 + 1 = 2$$

**Step 2: Determine the Region of Integration $R$** The boundary curves are: - Lower boundary: $y = x^2$ - Upper boundary: $y = \sqrt{x}$ (from $y^2 = x$) Intersection points: $$x^2 = \sqrt{x} \implies x^4 = x \implies x(x^3 - 1) = 0 \implies x = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad x = 1$$ The curves intersect at $(0, 0)$ and $(1, 1)$.

**Step 3: Evaluate the Double Integral** Applying Green's theorem: $$I = \iint_R 2 \, dx \, dy = 2 \iint_R dx \, dy = 2 \times (\text{Area of region } R)$$ Set up the iterated integral: $$\text{Area}(R) = \int_{x=0}^1 \left[ \int_{y=x^2}^{\sqrt{x}} dy \right] dx = \int_0^1 (\sqrt{x} - x^2) \, dx$$ Evaluate the single integral: $$\int_0^1 (x^{1/2} - x^2) \, dx = \left[ \frac{x^{3/2}}{3/2} - \frac{x^3}{3} \right]_0^1 = \left[ \frac{2}{3}x^{3/2} - \frac{1}{3}x^3 \right]_0^1 = \frac{2}{3} - \frac{1}{3} = \frac{1}{3}$$ Multiplying by the integrand value 2: $$I = 2 \left( \frac{1}{3} \right) = \frac{2}{3}$$

Question 12 3 Marks (Short Answer)

Q12. State Gauss divergence theorem and explain its physical significance in electrostatics and fluid mechanics. Q12. State Gauss divergence theorem and explain its physical significance in electrostatics and fluid mechanics.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\iint_S \vec{F} \cdot \hat{n} \, dS = \iiint_V (\nabla \cdot \vec{F}) \, dV \iff \Phi_{\text{net}} = \iiint (\text{source density}) \, dV$$

Gauss Divergence Theorem: Relates outward flux through a closed boundary surface to the volumetric accumulation of divergence within the bounded domain; underpins Gauss\'s Law in electromagnetics.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\iint_S \vec{F} \cdot \hat{n} \, dS = \iiint_V (\nabla \cdot \vec{F}) \, dV \iff \Phi_{\text{net}} = \iiint (\text{source density}) \, dV$$

Gauss Divergence Theorem: Relates outward flux through a closed boundary surface to the volumetric accumulation of divergence within the bounded domain; underpins Gauss\'s Law in electromagnetics.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\iint_S \vec{F} \cdot \hat{n} \, dS = \iiint_V (\nabla \cdot \vec{F}) \, dV \iff \Phi_{\text{net}} = \iiint (\text{source density}) \, dV$$

Gauss Divergence Theorem: Relates outward flux through a closed boundary surface to the volumetric accumulation of divergence within the bounded domain; underpins Gauss\'s Law in electromagnetics.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

**Statement of Gauss Divergence Theorem:** Let $V$ be a bounded volume in three-dimensional space enclosed by a piecewise smooth, closed, orientable surface $S$. Let $\vec{F}(x, y, z)$ be a continuously differentiable vector field defined on $V$ and its boundary $S$. Then the net outward flux of $\vec{F}$ across the closed surface $S$ is identically equal to the volume integral of the divergence of $\vec{F}$ throughout the interior volume $V$: $$\iint_S \vec{F} \cdot \hat{n} \, dS = \iiint_V (\nabla \cdot \vec{F}) \, dV$$ where $\hat{n}$ is the outward-pointing unit normal vector to the surface element $dS$.

**Physical Significance in Engineering and Physics:**

1. **Fluid Dynamics (Continuity and Mass Conservation):** If $\vec{v}$ represents the velocity vector field of a fluid with density $\rho$, the vector $\vec{F} = \rho \vec{v}$ represents mass flux density. The surface integral $\iint_S (\rho \vec{v}) \cdot \hat{n} \, dS$ quantifies the net physical rate at which fluid mass is escaping outwards through the enclosing boundary $S$. The divergence $\nabla \cdot (\rho \vec{v})$ represents the volumetric source density (local rate of fluid creation or expansion per unit volume). If $\nabla \cdot \vec{F} > 0$, the point acts as a source; if $\nabla \cdot \vec{F} < 0$, it acts as a sink. The theorem proves that the total outward mass discharge across the boundary equals the sum total of all internal fluid sources minus sinks inside the volume.

2. **Electromagnetics (Gauss's Law):** In electrostatics, Maxwell's first equation states $\nabla \cdot \vec{D} = \rho_v$, where $\vec{D}$ is the electric flux density and $\rho_v$ is volume charge density. Applying the Gauss divergence theorem: $$\iint_S \vec{D} \cdot \hat{n} \, dS = \iiint_V (\nabla \cdot \vec{D}) \, dV = \iiint_V \rho_v \, dV = Q_{\text{enclosed}}$$ This establishes that the total outward electric flux passing through any closed Gaussian surface equals the total enclosed electric charge $Q_{\text{enclosed}}$.

**Statement of Gauss Divergence Theorem:** Let $V$ be a bounded volume in three-dimensional space enclosed by a piecewise smooth, closed, orientable surface $S$.
