⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\oint_C = \int_{C_1} + \int_{C_2} = \frac{19}{20} - 1 = -\frac{1}{20} = \iint_R (x - 2y) dy\,dx$$

Verification of Green\'s Theorem: Compute line integral along piecewise boundary curves ($C_1: y=x^2, C_2: y=x$) and compare against surface double integral of $(N_x - M_y) = x - 2y$; both equal $-1/20$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

We are required to verify Green's theorem in the plane for: $$\oint_C [(xy + y^2)\, dx + x^2\, dy]$$ where $C$ is the closed curve bounded by the line $y = x$ and the parabola $y = x^2$, traversed in the positive (counter-clockwise) direction.

Green's theorem states: $$\oint_C (M \, dx + N \, dy) = \iint_R \left( \frac{\partial N}{\partial x} - \frac{\partial M}{\partial y} \right) dx \, dy$$

--- ### Part 1: Evaluation of the Double Integral (Right-Hand Side)

Here: $$M(x, y) = xy + y^2 \implies \frac{\partial M}{\partial y} = x + 2y$$ $$N(x, y) = x^2 \implies \frac{\partial N}{\partial x} = 2x$$ Compute the integrand: $$\frac{\partial N}{\partial x} - \frac{\partial M}{\partial y} = 2x - (x + 2y) = x - 2y$$

The region $R$ is bounded below by the parabola $y = x^2$ and above by the line $y = x$, from $x = 0$ to $x = 1$. $$\iint_R (x - 2y) \, dy \, dx = \int_{x=0}^1 \left[ \int_{y=x^2}^x (x - 2y) \, dy \right] dx$$

Evaluate the inner integral with respect to $y$: $$\int_{x^2}^x (x - 2y) \, dy = \left[ xy - y^2 \right]_{y=x^2}^{y=x}$$ $$= [x(x) - x^2] - [x(x^2) - (x^2)^2] = [x^2 - x^2] - [x^3 - x^4] = -(x^3 - x^4) = x^4 - x^3$$

Now evaluate the outer integral with respect to $x$: $$\int_0^1 (x^4 - x^3) \, dx = \left[ \frac{x^5}{5} - \frac{x^4}{4} \right]_0^1 = \frac{1}{5} - \frac{1}{4} = \frac{4 - 5}{20} = -\frac{1}{20}$$ Thus: $$\text{RHS} = -\frac{1}{20}$$

--- ### Part 2: Evaluation of the Line Integral (Left-Hand Side)

The closed boundary curve $C$ consists of two distinct oriented segments: $C = C_1 + C_2$. 1. Curve $C_1$: Along the parabola $y = x^2$ from the origin $(0, 0)$ to $(1, 1)$. 2. Curve $C_2$: Along the straight line $y = x$ from $(1, 1)$ back to the origin $(0, 0)$.

**Integral along $C_1$ ($y = x^2 \implies dy = 2x \, dx$, with $x$ running from $0$ to $1$):** $$\int_{C_1} [(xy + y^2)\, dx + x^2\, dy] = \int_0^1 \left[ \left( x(x^2) + (x^2)^2 \right) dx + x^2 (2x \, dx) \right]$$ $$= \int_0^1 \left( x^3 + x^4 + 2x^3 \right) dx = \int_0^1 (3x^3 + x^4) \, dx$$ $$= \left[ \frac{3x^4}{4} + \frac{x^5}{5} \right]_0^1 = \frac{3}{4} + \frac{1}{5} = \frac{15 + 4}{20} = \frac{19}{20}$$

**Integral along $C_2$ ($y = x \implies dy = dx$, with $x$ running from $1$ to $0$):** $$\int_{C_2} [(xy + y^2)\, dx + x^2\, dy] = \int_1^0 \left[ (x(x) + x^2)\, dx + x^2\, dx \right]$$ $$= \int_1^0 (x^2 + x^2 + x^2) \, dx = \int_1^0 3x^2 \, dx = \left[ x^3 \right]_1^0 = 0^3 - 1^3 = -1$$

**Total Line Integral:** $$\oint_C (M \, dx + N \, dy) = \int_{C_1} + \int_{C_2} = \frac{19}{20} + (-1) = \frac{19 - 20}{20} = -\frac{1}{20}$$ Thus: $$\text{LHS} = -\frac{1}{20}$$

Since $\text{LHS} = \text{RHS} = -\frac{1}{20}$, Green's theorem is completely verified.

We are required to verify Green's theorem in the plane for: $$\oint_C [(xy + y^2)\, dx + x^2\, dy]$$ where $C$ is the closed curve bounded by the line $y = x$ and the parabola $y = x^2$, traversed in the positive (counter-clockwise) direction.

Since $\text{LHS} = \text{RHS} = -\frac{1}{20}$, Green's theorem is completely verified.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\iiint_V (\nabla \cdot \vec{F}) dV = \int_0^1 \int_0^1 (4z - y) dydz = \frac{3}{2} = \sum_{i=1}^6 \iint_{S_i} \vec{F} \cdot \hat{n} \, dS$$

Gauss Theorem Verification on Cube: Evaluate outward normal flux across all 6 faces ($\Phi = 2 + 0 - 1 + 0 + 1/2 + 0 = 3/2$) and confirm identity with volume integral of divergence $4z - y = 3/2$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

We are required to verify the Gauss divergence theorem for the vector field: $$\vec{F} = 4xz\hat{i} - y^2\hat{j} + yz\hat{k}$$ over the unit cube bounded by the coordinate planes: $$x = 0, \quad x = 1, \quad y = 0, \quad y = 1, \quad z = 0, \quad z = 1$$

Gauss Divergence Theorem states: $$\iint_S \vec{F} \cdot \hat{n} \, dS = \iiint_V (\nabla \cdot \vec{F}) \, dV$$

--- ### Part 1: Evaluation of the Volume Integral (RHS)

Compute the divergence of $\vec{F}$: $$\nabla \cdot \vec{F} = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(4xz) + \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(-y^2) + \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(yz) = 4z - 2y + y = 4z - y$$

Evaluate the triple integral over the unit cube: $$\iiint_V (\nabla \cdot \vec{F}) \, dV = \int_{z=0}^1 \int_{y=0}^1 \int_{x=0}^1 (4z - y) \, dx \, dy \, dz$$ Since the integrand is independent of $x$, the $x$-integral is simply $\int_0^1 dx = 1$: $$= \int_{z=0}^1 \int_{y=0}^1 (4z - y) \, dy \, dz$$

Integrate with respect to $y$: $$\int_0^1 (4z - y) \, dy = \left[ 4zy - \frac{y^2}{2} \right]_0^1 = 4z(1) - \frac{1}{2} = 4z - \frac{1}{2}$$

Integrate with respect to $z$: $$\int_0^1 \left( 4z - \frac{1}{2} \right) dz = \left[ 2z^2 - \frac{z}{2} \right]_0^1 = 2(1)^2 - \frac{1}{2} = 2 - \frac{1}{2} = \frac{3}{2}$$ Therefore: $$\text{RHS} = \frac{3}{2}$$

--- ### Part 2: Evaluation of the Surface Integral over all Six Faces (LHS)

The closed boundary surface $S$ consists of the 6 square faces of the unit cube:

1. **Face $S_1$ (Right Face: $x = 1$, outward normal $\hat{n} = \hat{i}$, $dS = dy \, dz$):** $$\vec{F} \cdot \hat{n} = \vec{F} \cdot \hat{i} = 4xz = 4(1)z = 4z$$ $$I_1 = \int_0^1 \int_0^1 4z \, dy \, dz = \left( \int_0^1 dy \right) \left[ 2z^2 \right]_0^1 = (1)(2) = 2$$

2. **Face $S_2$ (Left Face: $x = 0$, outward normal $\hat{n} = -\hat{i}$, $dS = dy \, dz$):** $$\vec{F} \cdot \hat{n} = -(4xz) = -4(0)z = 0 \implies I_2 = 0$$

3. **Face $S_3$ (Front Face: $y = 1$, outward normal $\hat{n} = \hat{j}$, $dS = dx \, dz$):** $$\vec{F} \cdot \hat{n} = \vec{F} \cdot \hat{j} = -y^2 = -(1)^2 = -1$$ $$I_3 = \int_0^1 \int_0^1 (-1) \, dx \, dz = -1(1)(1) = -1$$

4. **Face $S_4$ (Back Face: $y = 0$, outward normal $\hat{n} = -\hat{j}$, $dS = dx \, dz$):** $$\vec{F} \cdot \hat{n} = -(-y^2) = y^2 = 0^2 = 0 \implies I_4 = 0$$

5. **Face $S_5$ (Top Face: $z = 1$, outward normal $\hat{n} = \hat{k}$, $dS = dx \, dy$):** $$\vec{F} \cdot \hat{n} = \vec{F} \cdot \hat{k} = yz = y(1) = y$$ $$I_5 = \int_0^1 \int_0^1 y \, dx \, dy = \left( \int_0^1 dx \right) \left[ \frac{y^2}{2} \right]_0^1 = (1)\left(\frac{1}{2}\right) = \frac{1}{2}$$

6. **Face $S_6$ (Bottom Face: $z = 0$, outward normal $\hat{n} = -\hat{k}$, $dS = dx \, dy$):** $$\vec{F} \cdot \hat{n} = -(yz) = -y(0) = 0 \implies I_6 = 0$$

**Total Surface Flux:** $$\iint_S \vec{F} \cdot \hat{n} \, dS = \sum_{i=1}^6 I_i = 2 + 0 + (-1) + 0 + \frac{1}{2} + 0 = 1 + \frac{1}{2} = \frac{3}{2}$$ Thus: $$\text{LHS} = \frac{3}{2}$$

Since $\text{LHS} = \text{RHS} = \frac{3}{2}$, the Gauss divergence theorem is verified.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

We are required to verify the Gauss divergence theorem for the vector field: $$\vec{F} = 4xz\hat{i} - y^2\hat{j} + yz\hat{k}$$ over the unit cube bounded by the coordinate planes: $$x = 0, \quad x = 1, \quad y = 0, \quad y = 1, \quad z = 0, \quad z = 1$$

Gauss Divergence Theorem states: $$\iint_S \vec{F} \cdot \hat{n} \, dS = \iiint_V (\nabla \cdot \vec{F}) \, dV$$

--- ### Part 1: Evaluation of the Volume Integral (RHS)

Compute the divergence of $\vec{F}$: $$\nabla \cdot \vec{F} = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(4xz) + \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(-y^2) + \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(yz) = 4z - 2y + y = 4z - y$$

Evaluate the triple integral over the unit cube: $$\iiint_V (\nabla \cdot \vec{F}) \, dV = \int_{z=0}^1 \int_{y=0}^1 \int_{x=0}^1 (4z - y) \, dx \, dy \, dz$$ Since the integrand is independent of $x$, the $x$-integral is simply $\int_0^1 dx = 1$: $$= \int_{z=0}^1 \int_{y=0}^1 (4z - y) \, dy \, dz$$

Integrate with respect to $y$: $$\int_0^1 (4z - y) \, dy = \left[ 4zy - \frac{y^2}{2} \right]_0^1 = 4z(1) - \frac{1}{2} = 4z - \frac{1}{2}$$

Integrate with respect to $z$: $$\int_0^1 \left( 4z - \frac{1}{2} \right) dz = \left[ 2z^2 - \frac{z}{2} \right]_0^1 = 2(1)^2 - \frac{1}{2} = 2 - \frac{1}{2} = \frac{3}{2}$$ Therefore: $$\text{RHS} = \frac{3}{2}$$

--- ### Part 2: Evaluation of the Surface Integral over all Six Faces (LHS)

The closed boundary surface $S$ consists of the 6 square faces of the unit cube:

1. **Face $S_1$ (Right Face: $x = 1$, outward normal $\hat{n} = \hat{i}$, $dS = dy \, dz$):** $$\vec{F} \cdot \hat{n} = \vec{F} \cdot \hat{i} = 4xz = 4(1)z = 4z$$ $$I_1 = \int_0^1 \int_0^1 4z \, dy \, dz = \left( \int_0^1 dy \right) \left[ 2z^2 \right]_0^1 = (1)(2) = 2$$

2. **Face $S_2$ (Left Face: $x = 0$, outward normal $\hat{n} = -\hat{i}$, $dS = dy \, dz$):** $$\vec{F} \cdot \hat{n} = -(4xz) = -4(0)z = 0 \implies I_2 = 0$$

3. **Face $S_3$ (Front Face: $y = 1$, outward normal $\hat{n} = \hat{j}$, $dS = dx \, dz$):** $$\vec{F} \cdot \hat{n} = \vec{F} \cdot \hat{j} = -y^2 = -(1)^2 = -1$$ $$I_3 = \int_0^1 \int_0^1 (-1) \, dx \, dz = -1(1)(1) = -1$$

4. **Face $S_4$ (Back Face: $y = 0$, outward normal $\hat{n} = -\hat{j}$, $dS = dx \, dz$):** $$\vec{F} \cdot \hat{n} = -(-y^2) = y^2 = 0^2 = 0 \implies I_4 = 0$$

5. **Face $S_5$ (Top Face: $z = 1$, outward normal $\hat{n} = \hat{k}$, $dS = dx \, dy$):** $$\vec{F} \cdot \hat{n} = \vec{F} \cdot \hat{k} = yz = y(1) = y$$ $$I_5 = \int_0^1 \int_0^1 y \, dx \, dy = \left( \int_0^1 dx \right) \left[ \frac{y^2}{2} \right]_0^1 = (1)\left(\frac{1}{2}\right) = \frac{1}{2}$$

6. **Face $S_6$ (Bottom Face: $z = 0$, outward normal $\hat{n} = -\hat{k}$, $dS = dx \, dy$):** $$\vec{F} \cdot \hat{n} = -(yz) = -y(0) = 0 \implies I_6 = 0$$

**Total Surface Flux:** $$\iint_S \vec{F} \cdot \hat{n} \, dS = \sum_{i=1}^6 I_i = 2 + 0 + (-1) + 0 + \frac{1}{2} + 0 = 1 + \frac{1}{2} = \frac{3}{2}$$ Thus: $$\text{LHS} = \frac{3}{2}$$

Since $\text{LHS} = \text{RHS} = \frac{3}{2}$, the Gauss divergence theorem is verified.

Q15. Verify Stokes' theorem for F = (2x - y)i - yz^2 j - y^2 z k over the upper hemisphere S: x^2 + y^2 + z^2 = 1, z >= 0 bounded by the circle C: x^2 + y^2 = 1 in the xy-plane. Q15. $$\nabla \times \vec{F} = \hat{k} \implies \iint_S (\nabla \times \vec{F}) \cdot \hat{n} \, dS = \iint_{x^2+y^2 \le 1} dx\,dy = \pi = \oint_C \vec{F} \cdot d\vec{r}$$

Stokes\' Theorem Verification: The line integral of $\vec{F}$ around the equator boundary circle equals $\pi$. The curl simplifies to unit vertical vector $\hat{k}$, whose surface flux projects directly to the equatorial disc area $\pi$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

We are required to verify Stokes' theorem for the vector field: $$\vec{F} = (2x - y)\hat{i} - yz^2\hat{j} - y^2 z\hat{k}$$ over the upper hemispherical surface $S: x^2 + y^2 + z^2 = 1, z \ge 0$, bounded by the circle $C: x^2 + y^2 = 1$ lying in the $xy$-plane ($z = 0$).

Stokes' Theorem states: $$\oint_C \vec{F} \cdot d\vec{r} = \iint_S (\nabla \times \vec{F}) \cdot \hat{n} \, dS$$

--- ### Part 1: Evaluation of the Line Integral around Boundary $C$ (LHS)

The boundary curve $C$ is the circle $x^2 + y^2 = 1, z = 0$ in the $xy$-plane, oriented counter-clockwise when viewed from above. Parametric equations of $C$: $$x = \cos t, \quad y = \sin t, \quad z = 0, \quad \text{where } 0 \le t \le 2\pi$$ The differentials are: $$dx = -\sin t \, dt, \quad dy = \cos t \, dt, \quad dz = 0$$

On $z = 0$, the vector field $\vec{F}$ simplifies to: $$\vec{F} = (2x - y)\hat{i} - y(0)^2\hat{j} - y^2(0)\hat{k} = (2x - y)\hat{i}$$ The differential displacement vector is $d\vec{r} = dx\hat{i} + dy\hat{j} + dz\hat{k}$. $$\vec{F} \cdot d\vec{r} = (2x - y) \, dx$$ Substitute the parametric relations: $$\vec{F} \cdot d\vec{r} = (2\cos t - \sin t)(-\sin t \, dt) = (-2\sin t \cos t + \sin^2 t) \, dt = (-\sin 2t + \sin^2 t) \, dt$$

Now integrate from $t = 0$ to $t = 2\pi$: $$\oint_C \vec{F} \cdot d\vec{r} = \int_0^{2\pi} (-\sin 2t + \sin^2 t) \, dt$$ 1. $\int_0^{2\pi} -\sin 2t \, dt = \left[ \frac{\cos 2t}{2} \right]_0^{2\pi} = \frac{1 - 1}{2} = 0$. 2. $\int_0^{2\pi} \sin^2 t \, dt = \int_0^{2\pi} \frac{1 - \cos 2t}{2} \, dt = \left[ \frac{t}{2} - \frac{\sin 2t}{4} \right]_0^{2\pi} = \frac{2\pi}{2} - 0 = \pi$.

Therefore: $$\text{LHS} = \oint_C \vec{F} \cdot d\vec{r} = 0 + \pi = \pi$$

--- ### Part 2: Evaluation of the Surface Integral of Curl (RHS)

Compute the curl of $\vec{F}$: $$\nabla \times \vec{F} = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ \frac{\partial}{\partial x} & \frac{\partial}{\partial y} & \frac{\partial}{\partial z} \\ 2x - y & -yz^2 & -y^2 z \end{vmatrix}$$

Evaluate components: - **$\hat{i}$-component:** $$\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(-y^2 z) - \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(-yz^2) = -2yz - (-2yz) = -2yz + 2yz = 0$$ - **$\hat{j}$-component:** $$-\left( \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(-y^2 z) - \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(2x - y) \right) = -(0 - 0) = 0$$ - **$\hat{k}$-component:** $$\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(-yz^2) - \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(2x - y) = 0 - (-1) = 1$$

Thus: $$\nabla \times \vec{F} = 0\hat{i} + 0\hat{j} + 1\hat{k} = \hat{k}$$

Now evaluate the surface integral: $$\iint_S (\nabla \times \vec{F}) \cdot \hat{n} \, dS = \iint_S \hat{k} \cdot \hat{n} \, dS$$ Recall from vector surface theory that $\hat{k} \cdot \hat{n} \, dS = dx \, dy$, which is the orthogonal projection of the infinitesimal surface patch $dS$ onto the $xy$-plane. As $S$ is the upper hemisphere $x^2 + y^2 + z^2 = 1, z \ge 0$, its projection onto the $xy$-plane is precisely the circular disk $R: x^2 + y^2 \le 1$. Therefore: $$\iint_S \hat{k} \cdot \hat{n} \, dS = \iint_R dx \, dy = \text{Area of the circular disk } (x^2 + y^2 \le 1) = \pi(1)^2 = \pi$$ Thus: $$\text{RHS} = \pi$$

Since $\text{LHS} = \text{RHS} = \pi$, Stokes' theorem is completely verified.

As $S$ is the upper hemisphere $x^2 + y^2 + z^2 = 1, z \ge 0$, its projection onto the $xy$-plane is precisely the circular disk $R: x^2 + y^2 \le 1$. Therefore: $$\iint_S \hat{k} \cdot \hat{n} \, dS = \iint_R dx \, dy = \text{Area of the circular disk } (x^2 + y^2 \le 1) = \pi(1)^2 = \pi$$ Thus: $$\text{RHS} = \pi$$

Since $\text{LHS} = \text{RHS} = \pi$, Stokes' theorem is completely verified.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

Stokes' Theorem states: $$\oint_C \vec{F} \cdot d\vec{r} = \iint_S (\nabla \times \vec{F}) \cdot \hat{n} \, dS$$

The boundary curve $C$ is the circle $x^2 + y^2 = 1, z = 0$ in the $xy$-plane, oriented counter-clockwise when viewed from above. Now integrate from $t = 0$ to $t = 2\pi$: $$\oint_C \vec{F} \cdot d\vec{r} = \int_0^{2\pi} (-\sin 2t + \sin^2 t) \, dt$$ 1. $\int_0^{2\pi} -\sin 2t \, dt = \left[ \frac{\cos 2t}{2} \right]_0^{2\pi} = \frac{1 - 1}{2} = 0$. 2. $\int_0^{2\pi} \sin^2 t \, dt = \int_0^{2\pi} \frac{1 - \cos 2t}{2} \, dt = \left[ \frac{t}{2} - \frac{\sin 2t}{4} \right]_0^{2\pi} = \frac{2\pi}{2} - 0 = \pi$.

Therefore: $$\text{LHS} = \oint_C \vec{F} \cdot d\vec{r} = 0 + \pi = \pi$$

--- ### Part 2: Evaluation of the Surface Integral of Curl (RHS)

Compute the curl of $\vec{F}$: $$\nabla \times \vec{F} = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ \frac{\partial}{\partial x} & \frac{\partial}{\partial y} & \frac{\partial}{\partial z} \\ 2x - y & -yz^2 & -y^2 z \end{vmatrix}$$

Evaluate components: - **$\hat{i}$-component:** $$\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(-y^2 z) - \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(-yz^2) = -2yz - (-2yz) = -2yz + 2yz = 0$$ - **$\hat{j}$-component:** $$-\left( \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(-y^2 z) - \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(2x - y) \right) = -(0 - 0) = 0$$ - **$\hat{k}$-component:** $$\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(-yz^2) - \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(2x - y) = 0 - (-1) = 1$$

Thus: $$\nabla \times \vec{F} = 0\hat{i} + 0\hat{j} + 1\hat{k} = \hat{k}$$

Now evaluate the surface integral: $$\iint_S (\nabla \times \vec{F}) \cdot \hat{n} \, dS = \iint_S \hat{k} \cdot \hat{n} \, dS$$ Recall from vector surface theory that $\hat{k} \cdot \hat{n} \, dS = dx \, dy$, which is the orthogonal projection of the infinitesimal surface patch $dS$ onto the $xy$-plane. As $S$ is the upper hemisphere $x^2 + y^2 + z^2 = 1, z \ge 0$, its projection onto the $xy$-plane is precisely the circular disk $R: x^2 + y^2 \le 1$. Therefore: $$\iint_S \hat{k} \cdot \hat{n} \, dS = \iint_R dx \, dy = \text{Area of the circular disk } (x^2 + y^2 \le 1) = \pi(1)^2 = \pi$$ Thus: $$\text{RHS} = \pi$$

Since $\text{LHS} = \text{RHS} = \pi$, Stokes' theorem is completely verified.

State Dirichlet's conditions for the existence of Fourier series of a function f(x) in an interval (c, c + 2L). What does the Fourier series converge to at a point of finite discontinuity?

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$S(x_0) = \begin{cases} f(x_0) & \text{at points of continuity} \\ \frac{f(x_0^+) + f(x_0^-)}{2} & \text{at points of jump discontinuity} \end{cases}$$

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$S(x_0) = \begin{cases} f(x_0) & \text{at points of continuity} \\ \frac{f(x_0^+) + f(x_0^-)}{2} & \text{at points of jump discontinuity} \end{cases}$$

Dirichlet\'s Conditions & Discontinuity Convergence: Requires boundedness, finite extrema, and finite jump discontinuities. At any step discontinuity, the Fourier series converges strictly to the midpoint of the jump.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

**Dirichlet's Conditions for the Existence of a Fourier Series:** A periodic function $f(x)$ of period $2L$ defined in an interval $(c, c + 2L)$ can be represented by a convergent Fourier series if it satisfies the following three classical conditions: 1. **Single-Valued and Bounded:** $f(x)$ is single-valued and bounded everywhere in the interval (there exists a real positive constant $M$ such that $|f(x)| \le M$). 2. **Piecewise Continuous:** $f(x)$ has at most a finite number of finite jump discontinuities in any single period. 3. **Finite Extrema:** $f(x)$ has at most a finite number of local maxima and local minima in any single period.

Functions satisfying these criteria are called piecewise smooth (Dirichlet) functions, which encompass virtually all physically realizable waveforms encountered in electrical and electronic engineering.

**Convergence of Fourier Series at Discontinuities:** Let $S(x)$ denote the sum of the Fourier series of $f(x)$ at any point $x$: 1. At any point of **continuity** $x_0$, the Fourier series converges exactly to the actual function value: $$S(x_0) = f(x_0)$$ 2. At a point of **finite jump discontinuity** $x = a$, the Fourier series converges strictly to the arithmetic mean (midpoint) of the right-hand and left-hand limits of $f(x)$ as $x$ approaches $a$: $$S(a) = \frac{f(a^+) + f(a^-)}{2} = \frac{1}{2} \left[ \lim_{h \to 0^+} f(a + h) + \lim_{h \to 0^+} f(a - h) \right]$$ 3. At the **endpoints** of the fundamental interval $[c, c + 2L]$, by periodicity, the Fourier series converges to: $$S(c) = S(c + 2L) = \frac{f(c^+) + f((c + 2L)^-)}{2}$$

**Dirichlet's Conditions for the Existence of a Fourier Series:** A periodic function $f(x)$ of period $2L$ defined in an interval $(c, c + 2L)$ can be represented by a convergent Fourier series if it satisfies the following three classical conditions: 1. **Single-Valued and Bounded:** $f(x)$ is single-valued and bounded everywhere in the interval (there exists a real positive constant $M$ such that $|f(x)| \le M$). 2. **Piecewise Continuous:** $f(x)$ has at most a finite number of finite jump discontinuities in any single period. 3. **Finite Extrema:** $f(x)$ has at most a finite number of local maxima and local minima in any single period.

Functions satisfying these criteria are called piecewise smooth (Dirichlet) functions, which encompass virtually all physically realizable waveforms encountered in electrical and electronic engineering.

**Convergence of Fourier Series at Discontinuities:** Let $S(x)$ denote the sum of the Fourier series of $f(x)$ at any point $x$: 1. At any point of **continuity** $x_0$, the Fourier series converges exactly to the actual function value: $$S(x_0) = f(x_0)$$ 2. At a point of **finite jump discontinuity** $x = a$, the Fourier series converges strictly to the arithmetic mean (midpoint) of the right-hand and left-hand limits of $f(x)$ as $x$ approaches $a$: $$S(a) = \frac{f(a^+) + f(a^-)}{2} = \frac{1}{2} \left[ \lim_{h \to 0^+} f(a + h) + \lim_{h \to 0^+} f(a - h) \right]$$ 3. At the **endpoints** of the fundamental interval $[c, c + 2L]$, by periodicity, the Fourier series converges to: $$S(c) = S(c + 2L) = \frac{f(c^+) + f((c + 2L)^-)}{2}$$

Question 17 3 Marks (Short Answer)
Short Answer ID: VCDET-Q17

Q17. Find the half-range Fourier sine series expansion for f(x) = k (constant) in the interval 0 < x < pi. Q17. Find the half-range Fourier sine series expansion for f(x) = k (constant) in the interval 0 < x < pi. Q17. Find the half-range Fourier sine series expansion for f(x) = k (constant) in the interval 0 < x < pi.

Watch Video Solution
🏛️
KTU Exam Source: June 2023 University Exam (MAT102) • Original Paper Location: Part A • Question 8 (3 Marks)
👁️ View Question Paper
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$f(x) = \frac{4k}{\pi} \sum_{n=1,3,5,\dots}^\infty \frac{\sin nx}{n} = \frac{4k}{\pi}\left(\sin x + \frac{\sin 3x}{3} + \frac{\sin 5x}{5} + \dots\right)$$

Half-Range Sine Series: All even harmonic coefficients vanish because $1 - (-1)^n = 0$. Odd harmonics decay as $1/n$, generating the classic synthesis of a constant step via sinusoidal basis modes.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$f(x) = \frac{4k}{\pi} \sum_{n=1,3,5,\dots}^\infty \frac{\sin nx}{n} = \frac{4k}{\pi}\left(\sin x + \frac{\sin 3x}{3} + \frac{\sin 5x}{5} + \dots\right)$$

Half-Range Sine Series: All even harmonic coefficients vanish because $1 - (-1)^n = 0$. Odd harmonics decay as $1/n$, generating the classic synthesis of a constant step via sinusoidal basis modes.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$f(x) = \frac{4k}{\pi} \sum_{n=1,3,5,\dots}^\infty \frac{\sin nx}{n} = \frac{4k}{\pi}\left(\sin x + \frac{\sin 3x}{3} + \frac{\sin 5x}{5} + \dots\right)$$

Half-Range Sine Series: All even harmonic coefficients vanish because $1 - (-1)^n = 0$. Odd harmonics decay as $1/n$, generating the classic synthesis of a constant step via sinusoidal basis modes.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

We are required to determine the half-range Fourier sine series expansion for the constant function $f(x) = k$ over the interval $0 < x < \pi$.

**Step 1: Formula for Half-Range Sine Series** The half-range sine series of $f(x)$ in $(0, \pi)$ is generated by extending $f(x)$ as an odd periodic function of period $2\pi$ into $(-\pi, \pi)$. Because the extension is odd, all cosine coefficients $a_0 = 0$ and $a_n = 0$. The series is given by: $$f(x) = \sum_{n=1}^\infty b_n \sin nx$$ where the Fourier sine coefficients $b_n$ are given by: $$b_n = \frac{2}{\pi} \int_0^\pi f(x) \sin nx \, dx$$

**Step 2: Compute the Coefficient $b_n$** Substitute $f(x) = k$: $$b_n = \frac{2}{\pi} \int_0^\pi k \sin nx \, dx = \frac{2k}{\pi} \int_0^\pi \sin nx \, dx$$ Evaluate the integral: $$b_n = \frac{2k}{\pi} \left[ \frac{-\cos nx}{n} \right]_0^\pi = \frac{2k}{n\pi} [-\cos n\pi - (-\cos 0)] = \frac{2k}{n\pi} [1 - \cos n\pi]$$ Recall that $\cos n\pi = (-1)^n$ for integer $n$: $$b_n = \frac{2k}{n\pi} [1 - (-1)^n]$$

**Step 3: Analyze Even and Odd Harmonics** - For **even** values of $n$ ($n = 2, 4, 6, \dots$): $$(-1)^n = 1 \implies 1 - (-1)^n = 1 - 1 = 0 \implies b_n = 0$$ - For **odd** values of $n$ ($n = 1, 3, 5, \dots$): $$(-1)^n = -1 \implies 1 - (-1)^n = 1 - (-1) = 2 \implies b_n = \frac{2k}{n\pi}(2) = \frac{4k}{n\pi}$$

Let $n = 2m - 1$ where $m = 1, 2, 3, \dots$: $$b_{2m-1} = \frac{4k}{(2m - 1)\pi}$$

**Step 4: Formulate the Complete Series** $$f(x) = \frac{4k}{\pi} \sum_{m=1}^\infty \frac{\sin(2m - 1)x}{2m - 1} = \frac{4k}{\pi} \left( \sin x + \frac{\sin 3x}{3} + \frac{\sin 5x}{5} + \frac{\sin 7x}{7} + \dots \right)$$

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

We are required to determine the half-range Fourier sine series expansion for the constant function $f(x) = k$ over the interval $0 < x < \pi$.

**Step 1: Formula for Half-Range Sine Series** The half-range sine series of $f(x)$ in $(0, \pi)$ is generated by extending $f(x)$ as an odd periodic function of period $2\pi$ into $(-\pi, \pi)$. Because the extension is odd, all cosine coefficients $a_0 = 0$ and $a_n = 0$. The series is given by: $$f(x) = \sum_{n=1}^\infty b_n \sin nx$$ where the Fourier sine coefficients $b_n$ are given by: $$b_n = \frac{2}{\pi} \int_0^\pi f(x) \sin nx \, dx$$

**Step 2: Compute the Coefficient $b_n$** Substitute $f(x) = k$: $$b_n = \frac{2}{\pi} \int_0^\pi k \sin nx \, dx = \frac{2k}{\pi} \int_0^\pi \sin nx \, dx$$ Evaluate the integral: $$b_n = \frac{2k}{\pi} \left[ \frac{-\cos nx}{n} \right]_0^\pi = \frac{2k}{n\pi} [-\cos n\pi - (-\cos 0)] = \frac{2k}{n\pi} [1 - \cos n\pi]$$ Recall that $\cos n\pi = (-1)^n$ for integer $n$: $$b_n = \frac{2k}{n\pi} [1 - (-1)^n]$$

**Step 3: Analyze Even and Odd Harmonics** - For **even** values of $n$ ($n = 2, 4, 6, \dots$): $$(-1)^n = 1 \implies 1 - (-1)^n = 1 - 1 = 0 \implies b_n = 0$$ - For **odd** values of $n$ ($n = 1, 3, 5, \dots$): $$(-1)^n = -1 \implies 1 - (-1)^n = 1 - (-1) = 2 \implies b_n = \frac{2k}{n\pi}(2) = \frac{4k}{n\pi}$$

Let $n = 2m - 1$ where $m = 1, 2, 3, \dots$: $$b_{2m-1} = \frac{4k}{(2m - 1)\pi}$$

**Step 4: Formulate the Complete Series** $$f(x) = \frac{4k}{\pi} \sum_{m=1}^\infty \frac{\sin(2m - 1)x}{2m - 1} = \frac{4k}{\pi} \left( \sin x + \frac{\sin 3x}{3} + \frac{\sin 5x}{5} + \frac{\sin 7x}{7} + \dots \right)$$

Question 18 7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Analytical Problem ID: VCDET-Q18

Q18. Find the Fourier series of f(x) = x^2 in the interval -pi <= x <= pi. Hence deduce that: (i) sum_{n=1}^infinity (-1)^(n-1)/n^2 = pi^2/12, and (ii) sum_{n=1}^infinity 1/n^2 = pi^2/6. Q18. Find the Fourier series of f(x) = x^2 in the interval -pi <= x <= pi. Hence deduce that: (i) sum_{n=1}^infinity (-1)^(n-1)/n^2 = pi^2/12, and (ii) sum_{n=1}^infinity 1/n^2 = pi^2/6. Q18. Find the Fourier series of f(x) = x^2 in the interval -pi <= x <= pi. Hence deduce that: (i) sum_{n=1}^infinity (-1)^(n-1)/n^2 = pi^2/12, and (ii) sum_{n=1}^infinity 1/n^2 = pi^2/6.

Watch Video Solution
🏛️
KTU Exam Source: June 2023 University Exam (MAT102) • Original Paper Location: Part B • Question 18(a) (7 Marks)
👁️ View Question Paper
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$x^2 = \frac{\pi^2}{3} + 4\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^n}{n^2}\cos nx \implies \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{1}{n^2} = \frac{\pi^2}{6}, \quad \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n^2} = \frac{\pi^2}{12}$$

Fourier Expansion of Parabola: For even function $x^2$, $b_n = 0, a_n = 4(-1)^n/n^2$. Evaluating at $x=0$ and $x=\pi$ resolves the famous Basel problem $\sum 1/n^2 = \pi^2/6$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$x^2 = \frac{\pi^2}{3} + 4\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^n}{n^2}\cos nx \implies \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{1}{n^2} = \frac{\pi^2}{6}, \quad \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n^2} = \frac{\pi^2}{12}$$

Fourier Expansion of Parabola: For even function $x^2$, $b_n = 0, a_n = 4(-1)^n/n^2$. Evaluating at $x=0$ and $x=\pi$ resolves the famous Basel problem $\sum 1/n^2 = \pi^2/6$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$x^2 = \frac{\pi^2}{3} + 4\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^n}{n^2}\cos nx \implies \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{1}{n^2} = \frac{\pi^2}{6}, \quad \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n^2} = \frac{\pi^2}{12}$$

Fourier Expansion of Parabola: For even function $x^2$, $b_n = 0, a_n = 4(-1)^n/n^2$. Evaluating at $x=0$ and $x=\pi$ resolves the famous Basel problem $\sum 1/n^2 = \pi^2/6$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

We are required to determine the Fourier series expansion of the quadratic function $f(x) = x^2$ in the symmetric interval $-\pi \le x \le \pi$, and deduce the following mathematical series: (i) $\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n^2} = 1 - \frac{1}{4} + \frac{1}{9} - \frac{1}{16} + \dots = \frac{\pi^2}{12}$ (ii) $\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{1}{n^2} = 1 + \frac{1}{4} + \frac{1}{9} + \frac{1}{16} + \dots = \frac{\pi^2}{6}$

**Step 1: Symmetry Analysis** Check if the function is even or odd: $$f(-x) = (-x)^2 = x^2 = f(x)$$ Because $f(x)$ is an **even function** over the symmetric interval $[-\pi, \pi]$: - All sine coefficients vanish identically: $b_n = 0$ for all $n \ge 1$. - The Fourier series consists strictly of cosine terms: $$f(x) = \frac{a_0}{2} + \sum_{n=1}^\infty a_n \cos nx$$

**Step 2: Determine $a_0$** $$a_0 = \frac{2}{\pi} \int_0^\pi f(x) \, dx = \frac{2}{\pi} \int_0^\pi x^2 \, dx = \frac{2}{\pi} \left[ \frac{x^3}{3} \right]_0^\pi = \frac{2}{\pi} \left( \frac{\pi^3}{3} \right) = \frac{2\pi^2}{3}$$ The constant term in the Fourier expansion is: $$\frac{a_0}{2} = \frac{\pi^2}{3}$$

**Step 3: Determine $a_n$** $$a_n = \frac{2}{\pi} \int_0^\pi x^2 \cos nx \, dx$$ Integrate by parts using Bernoulli's generalized formula: $$\int u v \, dx = u v_1 - u' v_2 + u'' v_3 - \dots$$ Let $u = x^2 \implies u' = 2x, u'' = 2, u''' = 0$. Let $v = \cos nx \implies v_1 = \frac{\sin nx}{n}, v_2 = -\frac{\cos nx}{n^2}, v_3 = -\frac{\sin nx}{n^3}$.

$$a_n = \frac{2}{\pi} \left[ x^2 \left(\frac{\sin nx}{n}\right) - 2x\left(-\frac{\cos nx}{n^2}\right) + 2\left(-\frac{\sin nx}{n^3}\right) \right]_0^\pi$$ $$a_n = \frac{2}{\pi} \left[ \frac{x^2 \sin nx}{n} + \frac{2x \cos nx}{n^2} - \frac{2\sin nx}{n^3} \right]_0^\pi$$

Evaluate at the limits $x = \pi$ and $x = 0$: Since $\sin n\pi = 0$ and $\sin 0 = 0$: $$a_n = \frac{2}{\pi} \left[ 0 + \frac{2\pi \cos n\pi}{n^2} - 0 - (0 + 0 - 0) \right] = \frac{2}{\pi} \left( \frac{2\pi (-1)^n}{n^2} \right) = \frac{4(-1)^n}{n^2}$$

**Step 4: Assemble the Fourier Series Expansion** $$x^2 = \frac{\pi^2}{3} + \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{4(-1)^n}{n^2} \cos nx = \frac{\pi^2}{3} - 4 \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n^2} \cos nx$$ $$x^2 = \frac{\pi^2}{3} - 4 \left( \frac{\cos x}{1^2} - \frac{\cos 2x}{2^2} + \frac{\cos 3x}{3^2} - \frac{\cos 4x}{4^2} + \dots \right)$$

--- ### Step 5: Deduce Series (i) and (ii)

**Deduction of (i): Substitute $x = 0$:** At $x = 0$, $f(x)$ is continuous and $\cos(0) = 1$: $$0^2 = \frac{\pi^2}{3} - 4 \left( \frac{1}{1^2} - \frac{1}{2^2} + \frac{1}{3^2} - \frac{1}{4^2} + \dots \right)$$ $$4 \left( 1 - \frac{1}{4} + \frac{1}{9} - \frac{1}{16} + \dots \right) = \frac{\pi^2}{3}$$ Divide both sides by 4: $$1 - \frac{1}{4} + \frac{1}{9} - \frac{1}{16} + \dots = \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n^2} = \frac{\pi^2}{12}$$

**Deduction of (ii): Substitute $x = \pi$:** At $x = \pi$, $\cos n\pi = (-1)^n$: $$\pi^2 = \frac{\pi^2}{3} + 4 \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^n (-1)^n}{n^2} = \frac{\pi^2}{3} + 4 \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{2n}}{n^2}$$ Since $(-1)^{2n} = ((-1)^2)^n = 1^n = 1$: $$\pi^2 = \frac{\pi^2}{3} + 4 \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{1}{n^2}$$ Subtract $\frac{\pi^2}{3}$ from both sides: $$\pi^2 - \frac{\pi^2}{3} = 4 \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{1}{n^2} \implies \frac{2\pi^2}{3} = 4 \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{1}{n^2}$$ Divide both sides by 4: $$\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{1}{n^2} = 1 + \frac{1}{4} + \frac{1}{9} + \frac{1}{16} + \dots = \frac{2\pi^2}{12} = \frac{\pi^2}{6}$$

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

We are required to determine the Fourier series expansion of the quadratic function $f(x) = x^2$ in the symmetric interval $-\pi \le x \le \pi$, and deduce the following mathematical series: (i) $\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n^2} = 1 - \frac{1}{4} + \frac{1}{9} - \frac{1}{16} + \dots = \frac{\pi^2}{12}$ (ii) $\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{1}{n^2} = 1 + \frac{1}{4} + \frac{1}{9} + \frac{1}{16} + \dots = \frac{\pi^2}{6}$

**Step 1: Symmetry Analysis** Check if the function is even or odd: $$f(-x) = (-x)^2 = x^2 = f(x)$$ Because $f(x)$ is an **even function** over the symmetric interval $[-\pi, \pi]$: - All sine coefficients vanish identically: $b_n = 0$ for all $n \ge 1$. - The Fourier series consists strictly of cosine terms: $$f(x) = \frac{a_0}{2} + \sum_{n=1}^\infty a_n \cos nx$$

**Step 2: Determine $a_0$** $$a_0 = \frac{2}{\pi} \int_0^\pi f(x) \, dx = \frac{2}{\pi} \int_0^\pi x^2 \, dx = \frac{2}{\pi} \left[ \frac{x^3}{3} \right]_0^\pi = \frac{2}{\pi} \left( \frac{\pi^3}{3} \right) = \frac{2\pi^2}{3}$$ The constant term in the Fourier expansion is: $$\frac{a_0}{2} = \frac{\pi^2}{3}$$

**Step 3: Determine $a_n$** $$a_n = \frac{2}{\pi} \int_0^\pi x^2 \cos nx \, dx$$ Integrate by parts using Bernoulli's generalized formula: $$\int u v \, dx = u v_1 - u' v_2 + u'' v_3 - \dots$$ Let $u = x^2 \implies u' = 2x, u'' = 2, u''' = 0$. Let $v = \cos nx \implies v_1 = \frac{\sin nx}{n}, v_2 = -\frac{\cos nx}{n^2}, v_3 = -\frac{\sin nx}{n^3}$.

$$a_n = \frac{2}{\pi} \left[ x^2 \left(\frac{\sin nx}{n}\right) - 2x\left(-\frac{\cos nx}{n^2}\right) + 2\left(-\frac{\sin nx}{n^3}\right) \right]_0^\pi$$ $$a_n = \frac{2}{\pi} \left[ \frac{x^2 \sin nx}{n} + \frac{2x \cos nx}{n^2} - \frac{2\sin nx}{n^3} \right]_0^\pi$$

Evaluate at the limits $x = \pi$ and $x = 0$: Since $\sin n\pi = 0$ and $\sin 0 = 0$: $$a_n = \frac{2}{\pi} \left[ 0 + \frac{2\pi \cos n\pi}{n^2} - 0 - (0 + 0 - 0) \right] = \frac{2}{\pi} \left( \frac{2\pi (-1)^n}{n^2} \right) = \frac{4(-1)^n}{n^2}$$

**Step 4: Assemble the Fourier Series Expansion** $$x^2 = \frac{\pi^2}{3} + \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{4(-1)^n}{n^2} \cos nx = \frac{\pi^2}{3} - 4 \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n^2} \cos nx$$ $$x^2 = \frac{\pi^2}{3} - 4 \left( \frac{\cos x}{1^2} - \frac{\cos 2x}{2^2} + \frac{\cos 3x}{3^2} - \frac{\cos 4x}{4^2} + \dots \right)$$

--- ### Step 5: Deduce Series (i) and (ii)

**Deduction of (i): Substitute $x = 0$:** At $x = 0$, $f(x)$ is continuous and $\cos(0) = 1$: $$0^2 = \frac{\pi^2}{3} - 4 \left( \frac{1}{1^2} - \frac{1}{2^2} + \frac{1}{3^2} - \frac{1}{4^2} + \dots \right)$$ $$4 \left( 1 - \frac{1}{4} + \frac{1}{9} - \frac{1}{16} + \dots \right) = \frac{\pi^2}{3}$$ Divide both sides by 4: $$1 - \frac{1}{4} + \frac{1}{9} - \frac{1}{16} + \dots = \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n^2} = \frac{\pi^2}{12}$$

**Deduction of (ii): Substitute $x = \pi$:** At $x = \pi$, $\cos n\pi = (-1)^n$: $$\pi^2 = \frac{\pi^2}{3} + 4 \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^n (-1)^n}{n^2} = \frac{\pi^2}{3} + 4 \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{2n}}{n^2}$$ Since $(-1)^{2n} = ((-1)^2)^n = 1^n = 1$: $$\pi^2 = \frac{\pi^2}{3} + 4 \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{1}{n^2}$$ Subtract $\frac{\pi^2}{3}$ from both sides: $$\pi^2 - \frac{\pi^2}{3} = 4 \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{1}{n^2} \implies \frac{2\pi^2}{3} = 4 \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{1}{n^2}$$ Divide both sides by 4: $$\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{1}{n^2} = 1 + \frac{1}{4} + \frac{1}{9} + \frac{1}{16} + \dots = \frac{2\pi^2}{12} = \frac{\pi^2}{6}$$

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

We are required to determine the Fourier series expansion of the quadratic function $f(x) = x^2$ in the symmetric interval $-\pi \le x \le \pi$, and deduce the following mathematical series: (i) $\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n^2} = 1 - \frac{1}{4} + \frac{1}{9} - \frac{1}{16} + \dots = \frac{\pi^2}{12}$ (ii) $\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{1}{n^2} = 1 + \frac{1}{4} + \frac{1}{9} + \frac{1}{16} + \dots = \frac{\pi^2}{6}$

**Step 1: Symmetry Analysis** Check if the function is even or odd: $$f(-x) = (-x)^2 = x^2 = f(x)$$ Because $f(x)$ is an **even function** over the symmetric interval $[-\pi, \pi]$: - All sine coefficients vanish identically: $b_n = 0$ for all $n \ge 1$. - The Fourier series consists strictly of cosine terms: $$f(x) = \frac{a_0}{2} + \sum_{n=1}^\infty a_n \cos nx$$

**Step 2: Determine $a_0$** $$a_0 = \frac{2}{\pi} \int_0^\pi f(x) \, dx = \frac{2}{\pi} \int_0^\pi x^2 \, dx = \frac{2}{\pi} \left[ \frac{x^3}{3} \right]_0^\pi = \frac{2}{\pi} \left( \frac{\pi^3}{3} \right) = \frac{2\pi^2}{3}$$ The constant term in the Fourier expansion is: $$\frac{a_0}{2} = \frac{\pi^2}{3}$$

**Step 3: Determine $a_n$** $$a_n = \frac{2}{\pi} \int_0^\pi x^2 \cos nx \, dx$$ Integrate by parts using Bernoulli's generalized formula: $$\int u v \, dx = u v_1 - u' v_2 + u'' v_3 - \dots$$ Let $u = x^2 \implies u' = 2x, u'' = 2, u''' = 0$. Let $v = \cos nx \implies v_1 = \frac{\sin nx}{n}, v_2 = -\frac{\cos nx}{n^2}, v_3 = -\frac{\sin nx}{n^3}$.

$$a_n = \frac{2}{\pi} \left[ x^2 \left(\frac{\sin nx}{n}\right) - 2x\left(-\frac{\cos nx}{n^2}\right) + 2\left(-\frac{\sin nx}{n^3}\right) \right]_0^\pi$$ $$a_n = \frac{2}{\pi} \left[ \frac{x^2 \sin nx}{n} + \frac{2x \cos nx}{n^2} - \frac{2\sin nx}{n^3} \right]_0^\pi$$

Evaluate at the limits $x = \pi$ and $x = 0$: Since $\sin n\pi = 0$ and $\sin 0 = 0$: $$a_n = \frac{2}{\pi} \left[ 0 + \frac{2\pi \cos n\pi}{n^2} - 0 - (0 + 0 - 0) \right] = \frac{2}{\pi} \left( \frac{2\pi (-1)^n}{n^2} \right) = \frac{4(-1)^n}{n^2}$$

**Step 4: Assemble the Fourier Series Expansion** $$x^2 = \frac{\pi^2}{3} + \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{4(-1)^n}{n^2} \cos nx = \frac{\pi^2}{3} - 4 \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n^2} \cos nx$$ $$x^2 = \frac{\pi^2}{3} - 4 \left( \frac{\cos x}{1^2} - \frac{\cos 2x}{2^2} + \frac{\cos 3x}{3^2} - \frac{\cos 4x}{4^2} + \dots \right)$$

--- ### Step 5: Deduce Series (i) and (ii)

**Deduction of (i): Substitute $x = 0$:** At $x = 0$, $f(x)$ is continuous and $\cos(0) = 1$: $$0^2 = \frac{\pi^2}{3} - 4 \left( \frac{1}{1^2} - \frac{1}{2^2} + \frac{1}{3^2} - \frac{1}{4^2} + \dots \right)$$ $$4 \left( 1 - \frac{1}{4} + \frac{1}{9} - \frac{1}{16} + \dots \right) = \frac{\pi^2}{3}$$ Divide both sides by 4: $$1 - \frac{1}{4} + \frac{1}{9} - \frac{1}{16} + \dots = \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n^2} = \frac{\pi^2}{12}$$

**Deduction of (ii): Substitute $x = \pi$:** At $x = \pi$, $\cos n\pi = (-1)^n$: $$\pi^2 = \frac{\pi^2}{3} + 4 \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^n (-1)^n}{n^2} = \frac{\pi^2}{3} + 4 \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{2n}}{n^2}$$ Since $(-1)^{2n} = ((-1)^2)^n = 1^n = 1$: $$\pi^2 = \frac{\pi^2}{3} + 4 \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{1}{n^2}$$ Subtract $\frac{\pi^2}{3}$ from both sides: $$\pi^2 - \frac{\pi^2}{3} = 4 \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{1}{n^2} \implies \frac{2\pi^2}{3} = 4 \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{1}{n^2}$$ Divide both sides by 4: $$\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{1}{n^2} = 1 + \frac{1}{4} + \frac{1}{9} + \frac{1}{16} + \dots = \frac{2\pi^2}{12} = \frac{\pi^2}{6}$$

Q19. Find the Fourier series of the periodic square wave: f(x) = -k for -pi < x < 0 and k for 0 < x < pi with period 2pi. Hence deduce the Gregory-Leibniz series for pi: 1 - 1/3 + 1/5 - 1/7 + ... = pi/4. Q19. Find the Fourier series of the periodic square wave: f(x) = -k for -pi < x < 0 and k for 0 < x < pi with period 2pi. Hence deduce the Gregory-Leibniz series for pi: 1 - 1/3 + 1/5 - 1/7 + ... = pi/4. Q19. Find the Fourier series of the periodic square wave: f(x) = -k for -pi < x < 0 and k for 0 < x < pi with period 2pi. Hence deduce the Gregory-Leibniz series for pi: 1 - 1/3 + 1/5 - 1/7 + ... = pi/4.

$$f(x) = \frac{4k}{\pi} \sum_{n=1,3,5,\dots}^\infty \frac{\sin nx}{n} \implies \text{at } x = \frac{\pi}{2}: \quad 1 - \frac{1}{3} + \frac{1}{5} - \frac{1}{7} + \dots = \frac{\pi}{4}$$

Square Wave Harmonic Spectrum: Contains purely odd harmonics with harmonic amplitudes decaying inversely as $1/n$. Evaluation at the peak $x = \pi/2$ directly establishes Leibniz\'s formula for $\pi$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$f(x) = \frac{4k}{\pi} \sum_{n=1,3,5,\dots}^\infty \frac{\sin nx}{n} \implies \text{at } x = \frac{\pi}{2}: \quad 1 - \frac{1}{3} + \frac{1}{5} - \frac{1}{7} + \dots = \frac{\pi}{4}$$

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$f(x) = \frac{4k}{\pi} \sum_{n=1,3,5,\dots}^\infty \frac{\sin nx}{n} \implies \text{at } x = \frac{\pi}{2}: \quad 1 - \frac{1}{3} + \frac{1}{5} - \frac{1}{7} + \dots = \frac{\pi}{4}$$

Square Wave Harmonic Spectrum: Contains purely odd harmonics with harmonic amplitudes decaying inversely as $1/n$. Evaluation at the peak $x = \pi/2$ directly establishes Leibniz\'s formula for $\pi$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

We are required to find the Fourier series of the periodic square wave: $$f(x) = \begin{cases} -k, & -\pi < x < 0 \\ k, & 0 < x < \pi \end{cases}$$ with period $2\pi$ ($f(x + 2\pi) = f(x)$), and hence deduce the Gregory-Leibniz series for $\pi$: $$1 - \frac{1}{3} + \frac{1}{5} - \frac{1}{7} + \dots = \frac{\pi}{4}$$

**Step 1: Test for Symmetry** Observe that: $$f(-x) = \begin{cases} -k, & 0 < -x < \pi \iff -\pi < x < 0 \\ k, & -\pi < -x < 0 \iff 0 < x < \pi \end{cases} = -f(x)$$ Because $f(-x) = -f(x)$ for all $x \in (-\pi, \pi) \setminus \{0\}$, $f(x)$ is a strictly **odd function**.

By the properties of odd functions over symmetric intervals: $$a_0 = 0$$ $$a_n = \frac{1}{\pi} \int_{-\pi}^\pi f(x) \cos nx \, dx = 0 \quad (\text{since } f(x)\cos nx \text{ is odd})$$ The Fourier series contains only sine terms: $$f(x) = \sum_{n=1}^\infty b_n \sin nx$$

**Step 2: Compute Fourier Sine Coefficient $b_n$** Using the property of odd functions ($f(x)\sin nx$ is even): $$b_n = \frac{2}{\pi} \int_0^\pi f(x) \sin nx \, dx = \frac{2}{\pi} \int_0^\pi k \sin nx \, dx = \frac{2k}{\pi} \left[ \frac{-\cos nx}{n} \right]_0^\pi$$ $$b_n = \frac{2k}{n\pi} [1 - \cos n\pi] = \frac{2k}{n\pi} [1 - (-1)^n]$$

- For **even** $n$ ($n = 2, 4, 6, \dots$): $$b_n = \frac{2k}{n\pi}[1 - 1] = 0$$ - For **odd** $n$ ($n = 1, 3, 5, \dots$): $$b_n = \frac{2k}{n\pi}[1 - (-1)] = \frac{4k}{n\pi}$$

**Step 3: Construct the Fourier Series Expansion** $$f(x) = \frac{4k}{\pi} \sum_{m=1}^\infty \frac{\sin(2m - 1)x}{2m - 1} = \frac{4k}{\pi} \left( \sin x + \frac{\sin 3x}{3} + \frac{\sin 5x}{5} + \frac{\sin 7x}{7} + \dots \right)$$

**Step 4: Deduction of the Leibniz Series at $x = \frac{\pi}{2}$** Notice that at $x = \frac{\pi}{2}$, $f(x)$ is continuous, and $f(\pi/2) = k$. Evaluate the harmonic sine terms at $x = \frac{\pi}{2}$: $$\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right) = 1$$ $$\sin\left(\frac{3\pi}{2}\right) = -1$$ $$\sin\left(\frac{5\pi}{2}\right) = 1$$ $$\sin\left(\frac{7\pi}{2}\right) = -1$$ In general, $\sin\left(\frac{(2m - 1)\pi}{2}\right) = (-1)^{m-1}$.

Substitute $x = \frac{\pi}{2}$ into the Fourier series: $$k = \frac{4k}{\pi} \left( 1 - \frac{1}{3} + \frac{1}{5} - \frac{1}{7} + \dots \right)$$ Divide both sides by $k$ (since $k \neq 0$): $$1 = \frac{4}{\pi} \left( 1 - \frac{1}{3} + \frac{1}{5} - \frac{1}{7} + \dots \right)$$ Multiply through by $\frac{\pi}{4}$: $$1 - \frac{1}{3} + \frac{1}{5} - \frac{1}{7} + \dots = \frac{\pi}{4}$$ This proves the celebrated Gregory-Leibniz formula.

$$\mathcal{F}_c\{e^{-ax}\} = \frac{a}{a^2 + \omega^2}, \quad \mathcal{F}_s\{e^{-ax}\} = \frac{\omega}{a^2 + \omega^2} \implies \int_0^\infty \frac{\cos(\omega x)}{a^2 + \omega^2} d\omega = \frac{\pi}{2a}e^{-ax}$$

Exponential Fourier Transforms: Cosine transform yields the Lorentzian spectrum $\frac{a}{a^2 + \omega^2}$. Applying inverse transform evaluates Laplace\'s famous contour integral $\frac{\pi}{2a}e^{-ax}$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\mathcal{F}_c\{e^{-ax}\} = \frac{a}{a^2 + \omega^2}, \quad \mathcal{F}_s\{e^{-ax}\} = \frac{\omega}{a^2 + \omega^2} \implies \int_0^\infty \frac{\cos(\omega x)}{a^2 + \omega^2} d\omega = \frac{\pi}{2a}e^{-ax}$$

Exponential Fourier Transforms: Cosine transform yields the Lorentzian spectrum $\frac{a}{a^2 + \omega^2}$. Applying inverse transform evaluates Laplace\'s famous contour integral $\frac{\pi}{2a}e^{-ax}$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\mathcal{F}_c\{e^{-ax}\} = \frac{a}{a^2 + \omega^2}, \quad \mathcal{F}_s\{e^{-ax}\} = \frac{\omega}{a^2 + \omega^2} \implies \int_0^\infty \frac{\cos(\omega x)}{a^2 + \omega^2} d\omega = \frac{\pi}{2a}e^{-ax}$$

Exponential Fourier Transforms: Cosine transform yields the Lorentzian spectrum $\frac{a}{a^2 + \omega^2}$. Applying inverse transform evaluates Laplace\'s famous contour integral $\frac{\pi}{2a}e^{-ax}$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

We are required to determine the Fourier cosine and Fourier sine transforms of the decaying exponential function $f(x) = e^{-ax}$ ($a > 0$), and hence evaluate the improper integral: $$\int_0^\infty \frac{\cos(\omega x)}{a^2 + \omega^2} \, d\omega$$

--- ### Part 1: Fourier Cosine Transform $\mathcal{F}_c\{e^{-ax}\}$

By definition: $$\mathcal{F}_c\{f(x)\} = F_c(\omega) = \int_0^\infty f(x) \cos(\omega x) \, dx = \int_0^\infty e^{-ax} \cos(\omega x) \, dx$$ Recall the standard exponential-trigonometric integration formula: $$\int e^{Ax} \cos Bx \, dx = \frac{e^{Ax}}{A^2 + B^2} (A \cos Bx + B \sin Bx)$$ Here $A = -a$ and $B = \omega$: $$F_c(\omega) = \left[ \frac{e^{-ax}}{a^2 + \omega^2} (-a \cos \omega x + \omega \sin \omega x) \right]_0^\infty$$

As $x \to \infty$, since $a > 0$, $e^{-ax} \to 0$ while the trigonometric terms remain bounded. Hence the upper limit vanishes ($0$). At $x = 0$, $e^0 = 1, \cos 0 = 1, \sin 0 = 0$: $$F_c(\omega) = 0 - \frac{1}{a^2 + \omega^2} (-a(1) + 0) = \frac{a}{a^2 + \omega^2}$$

--- ### Part 2: Fourier Sine Transform $\mathcal{F}_s\{e^{-ax}\}$

By definition: $$\mathcal{F}_s\{f(x)\} = F_s(\omega) = \int_0^\infty f(x) \sin(\omega x) \, dx = \int_0^\infty e^{-ax} \sin(\omega x) \, dx$$ Recall the formula: $$\int e^{Ax} \sin Bx \, dx = \frac{e^{Ax}}{A^2 + B^2} (A \sin Bx - B \cos Bx)$$ With $A = -a$ and $B = \omega$: $$F_s(\omega) = \left[ \frac{e^{-ax}}{a^2 + \omega^2} (-a \sin \omega x - \omega \cos \omega x) \right]_0^\infty$$ At $x \to \infty$, $e^{-ax} \to 0$. At $x = 0$, $e^0 = 1, \sin 0 = 0, \cos 0 = 1$: $$F_s(\omega) = 0 - \frac{1}{a^2 + \omega^2} (0 - \omega(1)) = \frac{\omega}{a^2 + \omega^2}$$

--- ### Part 3: Evaluation of the Improper Integral via Inverse Fourier Cosine Transform

By the Fourier Inverse Cosine Transform formula: $$f(x) = \frac{2}{\pi} \int_0^\infty F_c(\omega) \cos(\omega x) \, d\omega$$ Substitute $f(x) = e^{-ax}$ and $F_c(\omega) = \frac{a}{a^2 + \omega^2}$: $$e^{-ax} = \frac{2}{\pi} \int_0^\infty \frac{a}{a^2 + \omega^2} \cos(\omega x) \, d\omega = \frac{2a}{\pi} \int_0^\infty \frac{\cos(\omega x)}{a^2 + \omega^2} \, d\omega$$

Multiply both sides by $\frac{\pi}{2a}$ (for $a > 0$): $$\int_0^\infty \frac{\cos(\omega x)}{a^2 + \omega^2} \, d\omega = \frac{\pi}{2a} e^{-ax} \quad (x > 0, a > 0)$$ This formally evaluates Laplace's famous contour integral using Fourier transforms.

Question 21 3 Marks (Short Answer)
Short Answer ID: VCDET-Q21

Q21. Find the Laplace transform of f(t) = t^2 e^(-3t) sin 2t. Q21. Find the Laplace transform of f(t) = t^2 e^(-3t) sin 2t. Q21. Find the Laplace transform of f(t) = t^2 e^(-3t) sin 2t.

Watch Video Solution
🏛️
KTU Exam Source: June 2023 University Exam (MAT102) • Original Paper Location: Part A • Question 9 (3 Marks)
👁️ View Question Paper
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\mathcal{L}\{t^2 e^{-3t} \sin 2t\} = \frac{d^2}{ds^2}\left[\frac{2}{(s+3)^2 + 4}\right] = \frac{12s^2 + 72s + 92}{(s^2 + 6s + 13)^3}$$

Multiplication by $t^n$ & First Shifting: Applying the first shift property replaces $s \to s + 3$. Differentiating twice with respect to $s$ implements multiplication by $t^2$ in the time domain.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\mathcal{L}\{t^2 e^{-3t} \sin 2t\} = \frac{d^2}{ds^2}\left[\frac{2}{(s+3)^2 + 4}\right] = \frac{12s^2 + 72s + 92}{(s^2 + 6s + 13)^3}$$

Multiplication by $t^n$ & First Shifting: Applying the first shift property replaces $s \to s + 3$. Differentiating twice with respect to $s$ implements multiplication by $t^2$ in the time domain.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\mathcal{L}\{t^2 e^{-3t} \sin 2t\} = \frac{d^2}{ds^2}\left[\frac{2}{(s+3)^2 + 4}\right] = \frac{12s^2 + 72s + 92}{(s^2 + 6s + 13)^3}$$

Multiplication by $t^n$ & First Shifting: Applying the first shift property replaces $s \to s + 3$. Differentiating twice with respect to $s$ implements multiplication by $t^2$ in the time domain.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

We are required to evaluate the Laplace transform of the composite time function: $$f(t) = t^2 e^{-3t} \sin 2t$$

**Step 1: Elementary Transform** The Laplace transform of $\sin 2t$ is: $$\mathcal{L}\{\sin 2t\} = \frac{2}{s^2 + 2^2} = \frac{2}{s^2 + 4}$$

**Step 2: Apply the First Shifting (Frequency Translation) Property** The First Shifting Property states: $$\mathcal{L}\{e^{at} g(t)\} = G(s - a), \quad \text{where } G(s) = \mathcal{L}\{g(t)\}$$ Here $a = -3$, so we replace $s$ by $s - (-3) = s + 3$: $$\mathcal{L}\{e^{-3t} \sin 2t\} = \frac{2}{(s + 3)^2 + 4} = \frac{2}{s^2 + 6s + 9 + 4} = \frac{2}{s^2 + 6s + 13}$$ Let $H(s) = \frac{2}{s^2 + 6s + 13} = 2(s^2 + 6s + 13)^{-1}$.

**Step 3: Apply the Multiplication by $t^n$ Property** The multiplication by power of $t$ property states: $$\mathcal{L}\{t^n g(t)\} = (-1)^n \frac{d^n}{ds^n} G(s)$$ For $n = 2$: $$\mathcal{L}\{t^2 [e^{-3t} \sin 2t]\} = (-1)^2 \frac{d^2}{ds^2}[H(s)] = \frac{d^2}{ds^2}\left[ 2(s^2 + 6s + 13)^{-1} \right]$$

Compute the first derivative: $$\frac{d}{ds} H(s) = 2 \cdot (-1)(s^2 + 6s + 13)^{-2} (2s + 6) = -\frac{2(2s + 6)}{(s^2 + 6s + 13)^2} = -\frac{4(s + 3)}{(s^2 + 6s + 13)^2}$$

Compute the second derivative using the quotient rule: $$\frac{d^2}{ds^2} H(s) = -4 \frac{d}{ds}\left[ \frac{s + 3}{(s^2 + 6s + 13)^2} \right]$$ $$= -4 \left[ \frac{1 \cdot (s^2 + 6s + 13)^2 - (s + 3) \cdot 2(s^2 + 6s + 13)(2s + 6)}{(s^2 + 6s + 13)^4} \right]$$ Cancel the common factor $(s^2 + 6s + 13)$ from numerator and denominator: $$= -4 \left[ \frac{(s^2 + 6s + 13) - 2(s + 3)(2s + 6)}{(s^2 + 6s + 13)^3} \right]$$ Notice that $2s + 6 = 2(s + 3)$, so $2(s + 3)(2s + 6) = 4(s + 3)^2 = 4(s^2 + 6s + 9)$: $$= -4 \left[ \frac{s^2 + 6s + 13 - (4s^2 + 24s + 36)}{(s^2 + 6s + 13)^3} \right]$$ $$= -4 \left[ \frac{-3s^2 - 18s - 23}{(s^2 + 6s + 13)^3} \right] = \frac{4(3s^2 + 18s + 23)}{(s^2 + 6s + 13)^3} = \frac{12s^2 + 72s + 92}{(s^2 + 6s + 13)^3}$$

Therefore: $$\mathcal{L}\{t^2 e^{-3t} \sin 2t\} = \frac{12s^2 + 72s + 92}{(s^2 + 6s + 13)^3}$$

Question 22 3 Marks (Short Answer)
Short Answer ID: VCDET-Q22

Q22. State the Convolution Theorem for Laplace transforms. Use it to find L^(-1){1 / (s(s^2 + 1))}. Q22. State the Convolution Theorem for Laplace transforms. Use it to find L^(-1){1 / (s(s^2 + 1))}. Q22. State the Convolution Theorem for Laplace transforms. Use it to find L^(-1){1 / (s(s^2 + 1))}.

Watch Video Solution
🏛️
KTU Exam Source: June 2023 University Exam (MAT102) • Original Paper Location: Part A • Question 10 (3 Marks)
👁️ View Question Paper
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\{F(s)G(s)\} = \int_0^t f(u)g(t-u)du \implies \mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\frac{1}{s(s^2+1)}\right\} = \int_0^t \sin(t-u)du = 1 - \cos t$$

Convolution Theorem: Transforms multiplication in the complex $s$-frequency domain into a convolution integral in the temporal domain, enabling direct calculation without partial fractions.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\{F(s)G(s)\} = \int_0^t f(u)g(t-u)du \implies \mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\frac{1}{s(s^2+1)}\right\} = \int_0^t \sin(t-u)du = 1 - \cos t$$

Convolution Theorem: Transforms multiplication in the complex $s$-frequency domain into a convolution integral in the temporal domain, enabling direct calculation without partial fractions.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\{F(s)G(s)\} = \int_0^t f(u)g(t-u)du \implies \mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\frac{1}{s(s^2+1)}\right\} = \int_0^t \sin(t-u)du = 1 - \cos t$$

Convolution Theorem: Transforms multiplication in the complex $s$-frequency domain into a convolution integral in the temporal domain, enabling direct calculation without partial fractions.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

**Statement of the Convolution Theorem:** Let $F(s)$ and $G(s)$ be the Laplace transforms of functions $f(t)$ and $g(t)$ respectively, so that $\mathcal{L}^{-1}\{F(s)\} = f(t)$ and $\mathcal{L}^{-1}\{G(s)\} = g(t)$. Then the inverse Laplace transform of their algebraic product is given by the convolution integral: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\{F(s)G(s)\} = (f * g)(t) = \int_0^t f(u) g(t - u) \, du$$

**Problem Application:** We are required to evaluate: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ \frac{1}{s(s^2 + 1)} \right\}$$ using the convolution theorem.

**Step 1: Decompose into Product of Standard Transforms** Let: $$F(s) = \frac{1}{s} \implies f(t) = \mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\frac{1}{s}\right\} = 1$$ $$G(s) = \frac{1}{s^2 + 1} \implies g(t) = \mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\frac{1}{s^2 + 1}\right\} = \sin t$$

**Step 2: Apply the Convolution Integral** $$(f * g)(t) = \int_0^t f(u) g(t - u) \, du = \int_0^t (1) \sin(t - u) \, du$$

**Step 3: Evaluate the Integral** Perform integration with respect to the dummy variable $u$: $$\int_0^t \sin(t - u) \, du = \left[ \frac{-\cos(t - u)}{-1} \right]_{u=0}^{u=t} = \left[ \cos(t - u) \right]_{u=0}^{u=t}$$ Evaluate at the limits: $$= \cos(t - t) - \cos(t - 0) = \cos(0) - \cos(t) = 1 - \cos t$$ Therefore: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ \frac{1}{s(s^2 + 1)} \right\} = 1 - \cos t$$

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

**Statement of the Convolution Theorem:** Let $F(s)$ and $G(s)$ be the Laplace transforms of functions $f(t)$ and $g(t)$ respectively, so that $\mathcal{L}^{-1}\{F(s)\} = f(t)$ and $\mathcal{L}^{-1}\{G(s)\} = g(t)$. Then the inverse Laplace transform of their algebraic product is given by the convolution integral: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\{F(s)G(s)\} = (f * g)(t) = \int_0^t f(u) g(t - u) \, du$$

**Problem Application:** We are required to evaluate: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ \frac{1}{s(s^2 + 1)} \right\}$$ using the convolution theorem.

**Step 1: Decompose into Product of Standard Transforms** Let: $$F(s) = \frac{1}{s} \implies f(t) = \mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\frac{1}{s}\right\} = 1$$ $$G(s) = \frac{1}{s^2 + 1} \implies g(t) = \mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\frac{1}{s^2 + 1}\right\} = \sin t$$

**Step 2: Apply the Convolution Integral** $$(f * g)(t) = \int_0^t f(u) g(t - u) \, du = \int_0^t (1) \sin(t - u) \, du$$

**Step 3: Evaluate the Integral** Perform integration with respect to the dummy variable $u$: $$\int_0^t \sin(t - u) \, du = \left[ \frac{-\cos(t - u)}{-1} \right]_{u=0}^{u=t} = \left[ \cos(t - u) \right]_{u=0}^{u=t}$$ Evaluate at the limits: $$= \cos(t - t) - \cos(t - 0) = \cos(0) - \cos(t) = 1 - \cos t$$ Therefore: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ \frac{1}{s(s^2 + 1)} \right\} = 1 - \cos t$$

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

**Statement of the Convolution Theorem:** Let $F(s)$ and $G(s)$ be the Laplace transforms of functions $f(t)$ and $g(t)$ respectively, so that $\mathcal{L}^{-1}\{F(s)\} = f(t)$ and $\mathcal{L}^{-1}\{G(s)\} = g(t)$. Then the inverse Laplace transform of their algebraic product is given by the convolution integral: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\{F(s)G(s)\} = (f * g)(t) = \int_0^t f(u) g(t - u) \, du$$

**Problem Application:** We are required to evaluate: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ \frac{1}{s(s^2 + 1)} \right\}$$ using the convolution theorem.

**Step 1: Decompose into Product of Standard Transforms** Let: $$F(s) = \frac{1}{s} \implies f(t) = \mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\frac{1}{s}\right\} = 1$$ $$G(s) = \frac{1}{s^2 + 1} \implies g(t) = \mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\frac{1}{s^2 + 1}\right\} = \sin t$$

**Step 2: Apply the Convolution Integral** $$(f * g)(t) = \int_0^t f(u) g(t - u) \, du = \int_0^t (1) \sin(t - u) \, du$$

**Step 3: Evaluate the Integral** Perform integration with respect to the dummy variable $u$: $$\int_0^t \sin(t - u) \, du = \left[ \frac{-\cos(t - u)}{-1} \right]_{u=0}^{u=t} = \left[ \cos(t - u) \right]_{u=0}^{u=t}$$ Evaluate at the limits: $$= \cos(t - t) - \cos(t - 0) = \cos(0) - \cos(t) = 1 - \cos t$$ Therefore: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ \frac{1}{s(s^2 + 1)} \right\} = 1 - \cos t$$

Division by $t$ & Frullani-Type Integrals: Dividing by $t$ in time corresponds to integrating $F(u)$ from $s$ to $\infty$. Letting $s \to 0$ computes classic improper integrals like $\int_0^\infty \frac{\cos t - \cos 2t}{t} dt = \ln 2$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

We are required to evaluate the Laplace transform: $$\mathcal{L}\left\{ \frac{\cos at - \cos bt}{t} \right\}$$ and hence deduce the value of the improper integral: $$\int_0^\infty \frac{\cos t - \cos 2t}{t} \, dt$$

--- ### Part 1: Laplace Transform via Division by $t$ Property

**Division by $t$ Theorem:** If $\mathcal{L}\{f(t)\} = F(s)$ and $\lim_{t \to 0} \frac{f(t)}{t}$ exists finitely, then: $$\mathcal{L}\left\{ \frac{f(t)}{t} \right\} = \int_s^\infty F(u) \, du$$

**Check Existence of Limit at $t \to 0$:** $$\lim_{t \to 0} \frac{\cos at - \cos bt}{t} = \frac{1 - 1}{0} \quad \left(\frac{0}{0} \text{ form}\right)$$ Apply L'Hopital's rule: $$\lim_{t \to 0} \frac{-a \sin at + b \sin bt}{1} = 0$$ The limit exists finitely.

**Step 1: Transform of the Numerator** Let $f(t) = \cos at - \cos bt$. $$F(s) = \mathcal{L}\{\cos at - \cos bt\} = \frac{s}{s^2 + a^2} - \frac{s}{s^2 + b^2}$$

**Step 2: Integrate $F(u)$ from $s$ to $\infty$** $$\mathcal{L}\left\{ \frac{\cos at - \cos bt}{t} \right\} = \int_s^\infty \left( \frac{u}{u^2 + a^2} - \frac{u}{u^2 + b^2} \right) du$$ Recall $\int \frac{u}{u^2 + k^2} \, du = \frac{1}{2} \ln(u^2 + k^2)$: $$= \frac{1}{2} \left[ \ln(u^2 + a^2) - \ln(u^2 + b^2) \right]_s^\infty = \frac{1}{2} \left[ \ln\left( \frac{u^2 + a^2}{u^2 + b^2} \right) \right]_s^\infty$$

Evaluate at the limits: As $u \to \infty$: $$\lim_{u \to \infty} \frac{u^2 + a^2}{u^2 + b^2} = \lim_{u \to \infty} \frac{1 + a^2/u^2}{1 + b^2/u^2} = 1 \implies \ln(1) = 0$$ At the lower limit $u = s$: $$= 0 - \frac{1}{2} \ln\left( \frac{s^2 + a^2}{s^2 + b^2} \right) = \frac{1}{2} \ln\left( \frac{s^2 + b^2}{s^2 + a^2} \right)$$ Therefore: $$\mathcal{L}\left\{ \frac{\cos at - \cos bt}{t} \right\} = \frac{1}{2} \ln\left( \frac{s^2 + b^2}{s^2 + a^2} \right)$$

--- ### Part 2: Deduction of the Improper Integral

By the formal definition of the unilateral Laplace transform: $$\mathcal{L}\{g(t)\} = \int_0^\infty e^{-st} g(t) \, dt$$ Applying this to $g(t) = \frac{\cos at - \cos bt}{t}$: $$\int_0^\infty e^{-st} \left( \frac{\cos at - \cos bt}{t} \right) dt = \frac{1}{2} \ln\left( \frac{s^2 + b^2}{s^2 + a^2} \right)$$

Taking the limit as $s \to 0^+$: $$\int_0^\infty \frac{\cos at - \cos bt}{t} \, dt = \lim_{s \to 0^+} \frac{1}{2} \ln\left( \frac{s^2 + b^2}{s^2 + a^2} \right) = \frac{1}{2} \ln\left( \frac{b^2}{a^2} \right) = \frac{1}{2} \ln\left[\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)^2\right] = \ln\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)$$

For the specific given integral, set $a = 1$ and $b = 2$: $$\int_0^\infty \frac{\cos t - \cos 2t}{t} \, dt = \ln\left(\frac{2}{1}\right) = \ln 2$$

Taking the limit as $s \to 0^+$: $$\int_0^\infty \frac{\cos at - \cos bt}{t} \, dt = \lim_{s \to 0^+} \frac{1}{2} \ln\left( \frac{s^2 + b^2}{s^2 + a^2} \right) = \frac{1}{2} \ln\left( \frac{b^2}{a^2} \right) = \frac{1}{2} \ln\left[\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)^2\right] = \ln\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)$$

Q24. Using the Convolution Theorem, find the inverse Laplace transform of F(s) = 1 / (s^2 + a^2)^2. Q24. Using the Convolution Theorem, find the inverse Laplace transform of F(s) = 1 / (s^2 + a^2)^2. Q24. Using the Convolution Theorem, find the inverse Laplace transform of F(s) = 1 / (s^2 + a^2)^2.

🏛️
KTU Exam Source: June 2023 University Exam (MAT102) • Original Paper Location: Part B • Question 20(b) (7 Marks)
👁️ View Question Paper
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\frac{1}{(s^2+a^2)^2}\right\} = \frac{1}{a^2}\int_0^t \sin(au)\sin(a(t-u))du = \frac{\sin at - at\cos at}{2a^3}$$

Convolution of Resonant Poles: Convolving $\frac{1}{a}\sin at$ with itself generates the secular term $t\cos at$. The result describes resonance growth in undamped harmonic oscillators.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\frac{1}{(s^2+a^2)^2}\right\} = \frac{1}{a^2}\int_0^t \sin(au)\sin(a(t-u))du = \frac{\sin at - at\cos at}{2a^3}$$

Convolution of Resonant Poles: Convolving $\frac{1}{a}\sin at$ with itself generates the secular term $t\cos at$. The result describes resonance growth in undamped harmonic oscillators.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\frac{1}{(s^2+a^2)^2}\right\} = \frac{1}{a^2}\int_0^t \sin(au)\sin(a(t-u))du = \frac{\sin at - at\cos at}{2a^3}$$

Convolution of Resonant Poles: Convolving $\frac{1}{a}\sin at$ with itself generates the secular term $t\cos at$. The result describes resonance growth in undamped harmonic oscillators.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

We are required to determine the inverse Laplace transform of: $$F(s) = \frac{1}{(s^2 + a^2)^2}$$ using the **convolution theorem**.

**Step 1: Factor $F(s)$ into Two Recognizable Transforms** Write: $$F(s) = \frac{1}{s^2 + a^2} \cdot \frac{1}{s^2 + a^2} = F_1(s) \cdot F_2(s)$$ where: $$F_1(s) = F_2(s) = \frac{1}{s^2 + a^2}$$

**Step 2: Find Individual Time-Domain Functions** Recall the elementary inverse transform: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ \frac{a}{s^2 + a^2} \right\} = \sin at \implies \mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ \frac{1}{s^2 + a^2} \right\} = \frac{1}{a} \sin at$$ Thus: $$f_1(t) = f_2(t) = \frac{1}{a} \sin at$$

**Step 3: Set up the Convolution Integral** By the convolution theorem: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\{F_1(s)F_2(s)\} = (f_1 * f_2)(t) = \int_0^t f_1(u) f_2(t - u) \, du$$ $$= \int_0^t \left( \frac{1}{a} \sin au \right) \left( \frac{1}{a} \sin a(t - u) \right) du = \frac{1}{a^2} \int_0^t \sin(au) \sin(at - au) \, du$$

**Step 4: Use Trigonometric Product-to-Sum Identity** Recall: $$\sin A \sin B = \frac{1}{2} [\cos(A - B) - \cos(A + B)]$$ Let $A = au$ and $B = at - au$: $$A - B = au - (at - au) = 2au - at$$ $$A + B = au + (at - au) = at$$ Therefore: $$\sin(au) \sin(at - au) = \frac{1}{2} [\cos(2au - at) - \cos(at)]$$

**Step 5: Perform Integration** $$f(t) = \frac{1}{a^2} \int_0^t \frac{1}{2} [\cos(2au - at) - \cos(at)] \, du = \frac{1}{2a^2} \int_0^t [\cos(2au - at) - \cos(at)] \, du$$ Integrate with respect to $u$ (notice that $\cos(at)$ is independent of $u$): $$f(t) = \frac{1}{2a^2} \left[ \frac{\sin(2au - at)}{2a} - u \cos(at) \right]_{u=0}^{u=t}$$

Evaluate at upper limit $u = t$: $$\frac{\sin(2at - at)}{2a} - t \cos(at) = \frac{\sin(at)}{2a} - t \cos(at)$$

Evaluate at lower limit $u = 0$: $$\frac{\sin(0 - at)}{2a} - 0 \cdot \cos(at) = \frac{\sin(-at)}{2a} = -\frac{\sin(at)}{2a}$$

Subtract the lower limit evaluation: $$\left[ \frac{\sin(at)}{2a} - t \cos(at) \right] - \left[ -\frac{\sin(at)}{2a} \right] = \frac{\sin(at)}{2a} - t \cos(at) + \frac{\sin(at)}{2a} = \frac{2\sin(at)}{2a} - t \cos(at) = \frac{\sin(at)}{a} - t \cos(at)$$

**Step 6: Final Algebraic Simplification** $$f(t) = \frac{1}{2a^2} \left( \frac{\sin at}{a} - t \cos at \right) = \frac{\sin at - at \cos at}{2a^3}$$ Therefore: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ \frac{1}{(s^2 + a^2)^2} \right\} = \frac{\sin at - at \cos at}{2a^3}$$

We are required to determine the inverse Laplace transform of: $$F(s) = \frac{1}{(s^2 + a^2)^2}$$ using the **convolution theorem**.

**Step 1: Factor $F(s)$ into Two Recognizable Transforms** Write: $$F(s) = \frac{1}{s^2 + a^2} \cdot \frac{1}{s^2 + a^2} = F_1(s) \cdot F_2(s)$$ where: $$F_1(s) = F_2(s) = \frac{1}{s^2 + a^2}$$

**Step 2: Find Individual Time-Domain Functions** Recall the elementary inverse transform: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ \frac{a}{s^2 + a^2} \right\} = \sin at \implies \mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ \frac{1}{s^2 + a^2} \right\} = \frac{1}{a} \sin at$$ Thus: $$f_1(t) = f_2(t) = \frac{1}{a} \sin at$$

**Step 3: Set up the Convolution Integral** By the convolution theorem: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\{F_1(s)F_2(s)\} = (f_1 * f_2)(t) = \int_0^t f_1(u) f_2(t - u) \, du$$ $$= \int_0^t \left( \frac{1}{a} \sin au \right) \left( \frac{1}{a} \sin a(t - u) \right) du = \frac{1}{a^2} \int_0^t \sin(au) \sin(at - au) \, du$$

**Step 4: Use Trigonometric Product-to-Sum Identity** Recall: $$\sin A \sin B = \frac{1}{2} [\cos(A - B) - \cos(A + B)]$$ Let $A = au$ and $B = at - au$: $$A - B = au - (at - au) = 2au - at$$ $$A + B = au + (at - au) = at$$ Therefore: $$\sin(au) \sin(at - au) = \frac{1}{2} [\cos(2au - at) - \cos(at)]$$

**Step 5: Perform Integration** $$f(t) = \frac{1}{a^2} \int_0^t \frac{1}{2} [\cos(2au - at) - \cos(at)] \, du = \frac{1}{2a^2} \int_0^t [\cos(2au - at) - \cos(at)] \, du$$ Integrate with respect to $u$ (notice that $\cos(at)$ is independent of $u$): $$f(t) = \frac{1}{2a^2} \left[ \frac{\sin(2au - at)}{2a} - u \cos(at) \right]_{u=0}^{u=t}$$

Evaluate at upper limit $u = t$: $$\frac{\sin(2at - at)}{2a} - t \cos(at) = \frac{\sin(at)}{2a} - t \cos(at)$$

Evaluate at lower limit $u = 0$: $$\frac{\sin(0 - at)}{2a} - 0 \cdot \cos(at) = \frac{\sin(-at)}{2a} = -\frac{\sin(at)}{2a}$$

Subtract the lower limit evaluation: $$\left[ \frac{\sin(at)}{2a} - t \cos(at) \right] - \left[ -\frac{\sin(at)}{2a} \right] = \frac{\sin(at)}{2a} - t \cos(at) + \frac{\sin(at)}{2a} = \frac{2\sin(at)}{2a} - t \cos(at) = \frac{\sin(at)}{a} - t \cos(at)$$

**Step 6: Final Algebraic Simplification** $$f(t) = \frac{1}{2a^2} \left( \frac{\sin at}{a} - t \cos at \right) = \frac{\sin at - at \cos at}{2a^3}$$ Therefore: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ \frac{1}{(s^2 + a^2)^2} \right\} = \frac{\sin at - at \cos at}{2a^3}$$

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

We are required to determine the inverse Laplace transform of: $$F(s) = \frac{1}{(s^2 + a^2)^2}$$ using the **convolution theorem**.

**Step 1: Factor $F(s)$ into Two Recognizable Transforms** Write: $$F(s) = \frac{1}{s^2 + a^2} \cdot \frac{1}{s^2 + a^2} = F_1(s) \cdot F_2(s)$$ where: $$F_1(s) = F_2(s) = \frac{1}{s^2 + a^2}$$

**Step 2: Find Individual Time-Domain Functions** Recall the elementary inverse transform: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ \frac{a}{s^2 + a^2} \right\} = \sin at \implies \mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ \frac{1}{s^2 + a^2} \right\} = \frac{1}{a} \sin at$$ Thus: $$f_1(t) = f_2(t) = \frac{1}{a} \sin at$$

**Step 3: Set up the Convolution Integral** By the convolution theorem: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\{F_1(s)F_2(s)\} = (f_1 * f_2)(t) = \int_0^t f_1(u) f_2(t - u) \, du$$ $$= \int_0^t \left( \frac{1}{a} \sin au \right) \left( \frac{1}{a} \sin a(t - u) \right) du = \frac{1}{a^2} \int_0^t \sin(au) \sin(at - au) \, du$$

**Step 4: Use Trigonometric Product-to-Sum Identity** Recall: $$\sin A \sin B = \frac{1}{2} [\cos(A - B) - \cos(A + B)]$$ Let $A = au$ and $B = at - au$: $$A - B = au - (at - au) = 2au - at$$ $$A + B = au + (at - au) = at$$ Therefore: $$\sin(au) \sin(at - au) = \frac{1}{2} [\cos(2au - at) - \cos(at)]$$

**Step 5: Perform Integration** $$f(t) = \frac{1}{a^2} \int_0^t \frac{1}{2} [\cos(2au - at) - \cos(at)] \, du = \frac{1}{2a^2} \int_0^t [\cos(2au - at) - \cos(at)] \, du$$ Integrate with respect to $u$ (notice that $\cos(at)$ is independent of $u$): $$f(t) = \frac{1}{2a^2} \left[ \frac{\sin(2au - at)}{2a} - u \cos(at) \right]_{u=0}^{u=t}$$

Evaluate at upper limit $u = t$: $$\frac{\sin(2at - at)}{2a} - t \cos(at) = \frac{\sin(at)}{2a} - t \cos(at)$$

Evaluate at lower limit $u = 0$: $$\frac{\sin(0 - at)}{2a} - 0 \cdot \cos(at) = \frac{\sin(-at)}{2a} = -\frac{\sin(at)}{2a}$$

Subtract the lower limit evaluation: $$\left[ \frac{\sin(at)}{2a} - t \cos(at) \right] - \left[ -\frac{\sin(at)}{2a} \right] = \frac{\sin(at)}{2a} - t \cos(at) + \frac{\sin(at)}{2a} = \frac{2\sin(at)}{2a} - t \cos(at) = \frac{\sin(at)}{a} - t \cos(at)$$

**Step 6: Final Algebraic Simplification** $$f(t) = \frac{1}{2a^2} \left( \frac{\sin at}{a} - t \cos at \right) = \frac{\sin at - at \cos at}{2a^3}$$ Therefore: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ \frac{1}{(s^2 + a^2)^2} \right\} = \frac{\sin at - at \cos at}{2a^3}$$

Solve the initial value problem using Laplace transform: d^2y/dt^2 + 4 dy/dt + 4y = 6 e^(-2t) subject to y(0) = -2 and y'(0) = 8. Q25. Solve the initial value problem using Laplace transform: d^2y/dt^2 + 4 dy/dt + 4y = 6 e^(-2t) subject to y(0) = -2 and y'(0) = 8.

⚡ Key Equation

Initial Value Problem via Laplace Transforms: Incorporates initial conditions automatically into algebraic equations in $s$. The repeated pole $(s+2)^3$ yields critical damping response terms $e^{-2t}, te^{-2t}$, and resonant forcing $t^2 e^{-2t}$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$Y(s) = -\frac{2}{s+2} + \frac{4}{(s+2)^2} + \frac{6}{(s+2)^3} \implies y(t) = (3t^2 + 4t - 2)e^{-2t}$$

Initial Value Problem via Laplace Transforms: Incorporates initial conditions automatically into algebraic equations in $s$. The repeated pole $(s+2)^3$ yields critical damping response terms $e^{-2t}, te^{-2t}$, and resonant forcing $t^2 e^{-2t}$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$Y(s) = -\frac{2}{s+2} + \frac{4}{(s+2)^2} + \frac{6}{(s+2)^3} \implies y(t) = (3t^2 + 4t - 2)e^{-2t}$$

Initial Value Problem via Laplace Transforms: Incorporates initial conditions automatically into algebraic equations in $s$. The repeated pole $(s+2)^3$ yields critical damping response terms $e^{-2t}, te^{-2t}$, and resonant forcing $t^2 e^{-2t}$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

We are required to solve the initial value ordinary differential equation problem: $$\frac{d^2y}{dt^2} + 4\frac{dy}{dt} + 4y = 6 e^{-2t}$$ subject to the initial conditions: $$y(0) = -2, \quad y'(0) = 8$$ using the Laplace transform method.

**Step 1: Take Laplace Transform on Both Sides** $$\mathcal{L}\{y''(t)\} + 4\mathcal{L}\{y'(t)\} + 4\mathcal{L}\{y(t)\} = 6\mathcal{L}\{e^{-2t}\}$$ Let $\mathcal{L}\{y(t)\} = Y(s)$. Recall the derivative transform properties: $$\mathcal{L}\{y'(t)\} = s Y(s) - y(0) = s Y(s) - (-2) = s Y(s) + 2$$ $$\mathcal{L}\{y''(t)\} = s^2 Y(s) - s y(0) - y'(0) = s^2 Y(s) - s(-2) - 8 = s^2 Y(s) + 2s - 8$$ Right-hand side: $$6\mathcal{L}\{e^{-2t}\} = \frac{6}{s + 2}$$

**Step 2: Form the Algebraic Equation in $Y(s)$** $$(s^2 Y(s) + 2s - 8) + 4(s Y(s) + 2) + 4 Y(s) = \frac{6}{s + 2}$$ $$(s^2 + 4s + 4)Y(s) + 2s - 8 + 8 = \frac{6}{s + 2}$$ $$(s + 2)^2 Y(s) + 2s = \frac{6}{s + 2}$$

**Step 3: Solve for $Y(s)$** $$(s + 2)^2 Y(s) = \frac{6}{s + 2} - 2s$$ Divide through by $(s + 2)^2$: $$Y(s) = \frac{6}{(s + 2)^3} - \frac{2s}{(s + 2)^2}$$

Express the second term with numerator centered at $(s + 2)$: $$-2s = -2(s + 2 - 2) = -2(s + 2) + 4$$ Substitute this into the second fraction: $$-\frac{2s}{(s + 2)^2} = \frac{-2(s + 2) + 4}{(s + 2)^2} = -\frac{2}{s + 2} + \frac{4}{(s + 2)^2}$$

Combining all terms: $$Y(s) = -\frac{2}{s + 2} + \frac{4}{(s + 2)^2} + \frac{6}{(s + 2)^3}$$

**Step 4: Inverse Laplace Transform using First Shifting Theorem** Recall that: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ \frac{1}{(s + 2)^n} \right\} = e^{-2t} \mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ \frac{1}{s^n} \right\} = e^{-2t} \frac{t^{n-1}}{(n - 1)!}$$

1. First term: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ -\frac{2}{s + 2} \right\} = -2 e^{-2t}$$ 2. Second term: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ \frac{4}{(s + 2)^2} \right\} = 4 e^{-2t} \frac{t^{2-1}}{1!} = 4t e^{-2t}$$ 3. Third term: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ \frac{6}{(s + 2)^3} \right\} = 6 e^{-2t} \frac{t^{3-1}}{2!} = 6 e^{-2t} \frac{t^2}{2} = 3t^2 e^{-2t}$$

Summing all components: $$y(t) = -2e^{-2t} + 4t e^{-2t} + 3t^2 e^{-2t} = (3t^2 + 4t - 2)e^{-2t}$$

**Step 5: Verification of Initial Conditions** - Check $y(0)$: $$y(0) = (0 + 0 - 2)e^0 = -2 \quad (\text{Satisfied})$$ - Check $y'(0)$: $$y'(t) = (6t + 4)e^{-2t} - 2(3t^2 + 4t - 2)e^{-2t}$$ $$y'(0) = (0 + 4)(1) - 2(-2)(1) = 4 + 4 = 8 \quad (\text{Satisfied})$$

Recall the derivative transform properties: $$\mathcal{L}\{y'(t)\} = s Y(s) - y(0) = s Y(s) - (-2) = s Y(s) + 2$$ $$\mathcal{L}\{y''(t)\} = s^2 Y(s) - s y(0) - y'(0) = s^2 Y(s) - s(-2) - 8 = s^2 Y(s) + 2s - 8$$ Right-hand side: $$6\mathcal{L}\{e^{-2t}\} = \frac{6}{s + 2}$$

Express the second term with numerator centered at $(s + 2)$: $$-2s = -2(s + 2 - 2) = -2(s + 2) + 4$$ Substitute this into the second fraction: $$-\frac{2s}{(s + 2)^2} = \frac{-2(s + 2) + 4}{(s + 2)^2} = -\frac{2}{s + 2} + \frac{4}{(s + 2)^2}$$

Combining all terms: $$Y(s) = -\frac{2}{s + 2} + \frac{4}{(s + 2)^2} + \frac{6}{(s + 2)^3}$$

1. First term: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ -\frac{2}{s + 2} \right\} = -2 e^{-2t}$$ 2. Second term: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ \frac{4}{(s + 2)^2} \right\} = 4 e^{-2t} \frac{t^{2-1}}{1!} = 4t e^{-2t}$$ 3. Third term: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ \frac{6}{(s + 2)^3} \right\} = 6 e^{-2t} \frac{t^{3-1}}{2!} = 6 e^{-2t} \frac{t^2}{2} = 3t^2 e^{-2t}$$

Summing all components: $$y(t) = -2e^{-2t} + 4t e^{-2t} + 3t^2 e^{-2t} = (3t^2 + 4t - 2)e^{-2t}$$

**Step 5: Verification of Initial Conditions** - Check $y(0)$: $$y(0) = (0 + 0 - 2)e^0 = -2 \quad (\text{Satisfied})$$ - Check $y'(0)$: $$y'(t) = (6t + 4)e^{-2t} - 2(3t^2 + 4t - 2)e^{-2t}$$ $$y'(0) = (0 + 4)(1) - 2(-2)(1) = 4 + 4 = 8 \quad (\text{Satisfied})$$

We are required to solve the initial value ordinary differential equation problem: $$\frac{d^2y}{dt^2} + 4\frac{dy}{dt} + 4y = 6 e^{-2t}$$ subject to the initial conditions: $$y(0) = -2, \quad y'(0) = 8$$ using the Laplace transform method.

**Step 1: Take Laplace Transform on Both Sides** $$\mathcal{L}\{y''(t)\} + 4\mathcal{L}\{y'(t)\} + 4\mathcal{L}\{y(t)\} = 6\mathcal{L}\{e^{-2t}\}$$ Let $\mathcal{L}\{y(t)\} = Y(s)$. Recall the derivative transform properties: $$\mathcal{L}\{y'(t)\} = s Y(s) - y(0) = s Y(s) - (-2) = s Y(s) + 2$$ $$\mathcal{L}\{y''(t)\} = s^2 Y(s) - s y(0) - y'(0) = s^2 Y(s) - s(-2) - 8 = s^2 Y(s) + 2s - 8$$ Right-hand side: $$6\mathcal{L}\{e^{-2t}\} = \frac{6}{s + 2}$$

**Step 2: Form the Algebraic Equation in $Y(s)$** $$(s^2 Y(s) + 2s - 8) + 4(s Y(s) + 2) + 4 Y(s) = \frac{6}{s + 2}$$ $$(s^2 + 4s + 4)Y(s) + 2s - 8 + 8 = \frac{6}{s + 2}$$ $$(s + 2)^2 Y(s) + 2s = \frac{6}{s + 2}$$

**Step 3: Solve for $Y(s)$** $$(s + 2)^2 Y(s) = \frac{6}{s + 2} - 2s$$ Divide through by $(s + 2)^2$: $$Y(s) = \frac{6}{(s + 2)^3} - \frac{2s}{(s + 2)^2}$$

Express the second term with numerator centered at $(s + 2)$: $$-2s = -2(s + 2 - 2) = -2(s + 2) + 4$$ Substitute this into the second fraction: $$-\frac{2s}{(s + 2)^2} = \frac{-2(s + 2) + 4}{(s + 2)^2} = -\frac{2}{s + 2} + \frac{4}{(s + 2)^2}$$

Combining all terms: $$Y(s) = -\frac{2}{s + 2} + \frac{4}{(s + 2)^2} + \frac{6}{(s + 2)^3}$$

**Step 4: Inverse Laplace Transform using First Shifting Theorem** Recall that: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ \frac{1}{(s + 2)^n} \right\} = e^{-2t} \mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ \frac{1}{s^n} \right\} = e^{-2t} \frac{t^{n-1}}{(n - 1)!}$$

1. First term: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ -\frac{2}{s + 2} \right\} = -2 e^{-2t}$$ 2. Second term: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ \frac{4}{(s + 2)^2} \right\} = 4 e^{-2t} \frac{t^{2-1}}{1!} = 4t e^{-2t}$$ 3. Third term: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ \frac{6}{(s + 2)^3} \right\} = 6 e^{-2t} \frac{t^{3-1}}{2!} = 6 e^{-2t} \frac{t^2}{2} = 3t^2 e^{-2t}$$

Summing all components: $$y(t) = -2e^{-2t} + 4t e^{-2t} + 3t^2 e^{-2t} = (3t^2 + 4t - 2)e^{-2t}$$

**Step 5: Verification of Initial Conditions** - Check $y(0)$: $$y(0) = (0 + 0 - 2)e^0 = -2 \quad (\text{Satisfied})$$ - Check $y'(0)$: $$y'(t) = (6t + 4)e^{-2t} - 2(3t^2 + 4t - 2)e^{-2t}$$ $$y'(0) = (0 + 4)(1) - 2(-2)(1) = 4 + 4 = 8 \quad (\text{Satisfied})$$

Analytical Problem ID: VCDET-Q26

Q26. Solve the system of simultaneous ordinary differential equations using Laplace transform: dx/dt + y = sin t, dy/dt + x = cos t subject to x(0) = 1 and y(0) = 0. Q26. Solve the system of simultaneous ordinary differential equations using Laplace transform: dx/dt + y = sin t, dy/dt + x = cos t subject to x(0) = 1 and y(0) = 0. Q26. Solve the system of simultaneous ordinary differential equations using Laplace transform: dx/dt + y = sin t, dy/dt + x = cos t subject to x(0) = 1 and y(0) = 0.
